<a id="f28"></a>

### 28. do_query emits a "gone" event that 9.2's list of events does not contain

`src/dvs.c`:977 &middot; worth-recording &middot; false-doc-claim &middot; audit `m7`

**Status: fixed.** `do_query` emits `status` with a `gone` detail, which is an event 9.2 lists.

**Evidence.**

do_query on an unknown id does "emit_event(sw, parent->id, \"gone\", (dvs_id)target, NULL);". doc/Messaging.md:896-897 enumerates the vocabulary: "The events are `spawned`, `exited`, `faulted`, `exceeded`, `hibernated`, `throttled`,\n`denied`, and `status`." "gone" is not among the eight; in 9.5 `"gone"` is a push status, not an event name.

**Failure scenario.**

A supervisor written from 9.2 -- like the one in dvs_check.c:249, which branches on exited/faulted/exceeded and logs everything else -- queries an id that has already been reaped and receives an event kind its handler has no case for. Any supervisor validating e.event against the documented set treats a legitimate answer to its own query as a protocol error.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Emit "status" with a detail of "gone" (do_query already builds a detail string), or add `gone` to 9.2's list. Either way one of the two has to move.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=certain.**

I tried to refute this and could not. What I checked:

LOCATION IS WRONG. src/dvs.c:977 is `break;` inside do_hibernate's ancestry walk, not do_query. The emit the reporter quotes lives at src/dvs.c:1023, inside do_query (function opens at 1011). The quoted text is verbatim-accurate at 1023: `    emit_event(sw, parent->id, "gone", (dvs_id)target, NULL);`. Off by 46 lines and into a different function — but the code exists.

DOC QUOTE IS ACCURATE. doc/Messaging.md:896-897 reads exactly "The events are `spawned`, `exited`, `faulted`, `exceeded`, `hibernated`, `throttled`,\n`denied`, and `status`." I read all of 9.2 through 9.5 (doc lines 872-960) plus section 17's swarm corrections (~2540-2560) looking for the rescuing sentence a skeptic should expect. There is none. I grepped every occurrence of "gone" in the doc (396, 467, 469, 527, 543, 557, 565-566, 603, 937, 939, 1866, 1910-1912, 1928, 2316, 2661): every one is a push/endpoint status, never an event kind. The 9.5 row is "| Push to a dead instance | `\"gone\"`, immediately, never blocking |" — so the name is already claimed by a different vocabulary. I also grepped `spawned|throttled|`status`|e.event` across the doc: 896-897 is the only enumeration, and 897 goes on to justify additions to it ("`spawned` and `denied` are additions the draft's list implies but does not state"), which is exactly the framing that makes the list authoritative and closed rather than illustrative.

REACHABILITY TRACED. drain() dispatches `else if (strcmp(op, "query") == 0) do_query(...)` at src/dvs.c:1093-1094, gated only on DVS_CAP_LIFECYCLE (line 1050). do_query calls find() (defined at 147), which walks slots comparing `sw->slots[i].id == id`; release() at src/dvs.c:207 sets `sl->id = 0;  /* the slot is free; the handle is not reused */`, and kill_subtree calls release before emitting "exited". So the path is not exotic: a supervisor receives `exited` for a child, queries that id to read final usage, find() returns NULL, and it gets `{event="gone"}`. emit_event (502-545) writes the key literally as "event", so the guest sees e.event == "gone".

NOT COVERED ANYWHERE. I grepped test/, script/ and bindings/ for the behaviour rather than the name: no file sends `op = "query"` at all (the only "query" hits under test/ are T.querytab/T.querystr in the stock Lua suite plus a prose comment at dvs_check.c:873). So this is not the doc lagging a tested behaviour — the query op's event vocabulary is entirely unexercised, which is how the mismatch survived.

The cited supervisor is real and behaves as described — dvs_check.c:247 is `if e.event == 'exited' or e.event == 'faulted' or e.event == 'exceeded' then` with an unconditional log push at 246 — though it never issues a query, so the concrete harm is a doc/code disagreement rather than a crash. That still satisfies criterion (b): a verbatim doc sentence the code contradicts. Severity stays low, as the reporter said.

*Correction to the report:* Location is src/dvs.c:1023, not :977 (line 977 is `break;` in do_hibernate). Also narrow the failure scenario: no in-tree supervisor issues a query, so there is no live crash — the demonstrable defect is that doc/Messaging.md:896-897 states a closed set of eight event kinds while src/dvs.c:1023 emits a ninth, "gone", reachable whenever a lifecycle holder queries a reaped id (release() zeroes sl->id at src/dvs.c:207 before kill_subtree emits `exited`, so querying a just-exited child hits it immediately). Aggravating rather than incidental: 9.5 already binds "gone" to a push status, so the token now means two different things in two vocabularies. Fix is either adding `gone` to 9.2's list or emitting `status` with detail "gone" — do_query already builds a detail string at src/dvs.c:1026-1030.

<a id="f29"></a>

### 29. make verify_wasm names four files no target produces, and its first step reports success regardless because `| head` discards the pipeline status

`Makefile`:209 &middot; worth-recording &middot; test-passes-for-wrong-reason &middot; audit `single-point`

**Status: fixed.** The four file names are the ones the build writes, and `grep -q` replaces `| head -n 5`, so a missing symbol fails the recipe rather than being swallowed. **The target is still not on any workflow, and that is now a deliberate second decision rather than the original oversight.** It was added to `build.yml` and ran for the first time in the 5.5.1_build4 release run, where step 1 failed: `llvm-objdump -d` on `onelua_wasi.o` matches neither "setjmp" nor "longjmp". The artifact is fine -- the same run put the WASI module through wasmtime and the JS integration test's error/traceback case against real wasm, which is the unwinding path sjlj lowering exists for -- so the check was wrong. Step 1 now reads the symbol table with `llvm-nm` instead of disassembling with `objdump -d`, which is where a symbol name lives, and that is still unverified reasoning rather than a run. **The lesson is separate from the fix: a check that has never executed anywhere does not belong on the release-critical path on its first run.** It goes back only after it has passed once.

**Evidence.**

Makefile:208-216 define the three steps of `verify_wasm` (Makefile:260). Every path is wrong: :209 inspects `/data/onelua.o` but `_wasm_build_step1` (Makefile:102) emits `onelua_wasi.o`; :212 inspects `/data/wasm_stubs.o` but `_wasm_build_step2` (Makefile:110) emits `wasm_stubs_wasi.o`; :215-216 inspect `/data/diluvium.wasm` and `/data/libdiluvium.wasm` but `_wasm_build_step3` (Makefile:198-199) emits `diluvium_wasi.wasm` and `libdiluvium_wasi.wasm`. Separately, :209 and :215-216 end in `| head -n 5`, so the recipe's exit status is head's, and llvm-objdump/llvm-nm failing on a nonexistent file plus grep matching nothing still yields 0.

**Failure scenario.**

Step 1 is a check that can never fail: point `llvm-objdump` at any nonexistent file and `... | grep -E "longjmp|setjmp" | head -n 5` still exits 0, so "the wasm build still lowers setjmp onto exception handling" is asserted vacuously. The target as a whole then dies at :212, whose grep has no `head` to swallow it, so a developer running `make verify_wasm` gets a failure that has nothing to do with the code. No workflow references verify_wasm, so neither condition has ever been observed.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Fix the four filenames to the `_wasi` forms the build actually produces, drop the `| head -n 5` from :209 and :215-216 (or replace each pipeline with `... | grep -qE ...`), and add verify_wasm to whichever job runs `make build_wasm` so it is exercised rather than rotting.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=likely.**

I tried to refute this and could not. Every quote checks out at the stated line, and both mechanisms are verifiable by reading alone.

QUOTES CONFIRMED VERBATIM AT THE STATED LINES (/home/user/diluvium/Makefile):
  208  _wasm_verify_step1:
  209  	$(PODMAN_RUN_WASM) /opt/wasi-sdk/bin/llvm-objdump -d /data/onelua.o | grep -E "longjmp|setjmp" | head -n 5
  211  _wasm_verify_step2:
  212  	$(PODMAN_RUN_WASM) /opt/wasi-sdk/bin/llvm-nm /data/wasm_stubs.o | grep system
  214  _wasm_verify_step3:
  215  	$(PODMAN_RUN_WASM) /opt/wasi-sdk/bin/llvm-nm /data/diluvium.wasm | grep -E "luaL_newstate|lua_close" | head -n 5
  216  	$(PODMAN_RUN_WASM) /opt/wasi-sdk/bin/llvm-nm /data/libdiluvium.wasm | grep -E "luaL_newstate|lua_close" | head -n 5
  260  verify_wasm: _wasm_verify_step1 _wasm_verify_step2 _wasm_verify_step3
And the producers: :102 `-c onelua.c -o onelua_wasi.o`, :110 `-c wasm_stubs.c -o wasm_stubs_wasi.o`, :198/:199 `-o diluvium_wasi.wasm` / `-o libdiluvium_wasi.wasm`. So none of the four inspected paths is what `build_wasm` (:218) leaves in `.data`.

MECHANISM 1 (unfailable step) HOLDS. I grepped the whole Makefile for `SHELL`, `.SHELLFLAGS`, `.ONESHELL` and active `include` — none exist (:43's `-include script/platform.mk` is commented out). Recipes therefore run under plain `/bin/sh -c` with no `pipefail`, so a pipeline's status is its last command's. `head -n 5` exits 0 whether or not `llvm-objdump`/`grep` produced anything, so :209 and both lines of :215-216 cannot fail. :212 is the only one whose status is a `grep`'s, which is why the target dies there and not earlier — exactly as reported.

MECHANISM 2 (the paths are stale, and it is drift not design) HOLDS. `/data/diluvium.wasm` and `/data/libdiluvium.wasm` appear nowhere else in the tree except the commented-out `web_start` at :469. `git log -S` shows the verify targets arrived in f0a7561 when the wasm step did emit `onelua.o`, and commit 445e3b7 renamed the wasm outputs to the `_wasi` forms without touching :209-216.

NOTHING EXERCISES IT. `grep -rn verify_wasm` over the entire checkout returns only Makefile:208/211/214/260 (plus identical copies under .claude/worktrees/). `.github/workflows/{build.yml:88, test.yml:250, test.yml:303}` run `make build_wasm` and never `make verify_wasm`, so neither the vacuous pass nor the :212 abort has ever been observed. Confirms the reporter's claim rather than refuting it.

WHAT I CHECKED THAT DOES NOT SAVE IT. doc/Messaging.md never mentions `verify_wasm`, so no doc sentence rescues or is contradicted by this — the finding rests on branch (a), a demonstrable wrong result, and the reporter did not claim otherwise (the quoted "the wasm build still lowers setjmp onto exception handling" is their paraphrase of the check's intent, not a doc quote). The properties themselves are genuinely covered elsewhere — build.yml/test.yml load `dist/diluvium_wasi.wasm` in the wasmtime `sandboxed` example and `node test/instance.integration.mjs`, which is stronger than an `nm | grep` — so no real coverage is missing; what is broken is the target that claims to be the verification.

WHY NOT `certain`: the target is vestigial and unreferenced, so it propagates no false statement to a reader of the doc; the honest severity is the reporter's own "worth-recording". It clears the bar because it is not a risk judgment — it is two mechanically confirmed defects at exact lines, one of which is precisely the unfailable-check class convention 2 exists to catch.

*Correction to the report:* Two corrections to the finding as written, neither fatal.

(1) The title's "names four files no target produces" is wrong for half its subjects. `/data/onelua.o` and `/data/wasm_stubs.o` ARE produced — by `_native_static_lib` (Makefile:76-77, `cd .data && gcc ... -o onelua.o` / `-o wasm_stubs.o`) and by `_portable_static_lib` (Makefile:91-92, writing into the same `/data` mount). Only `/data/diluvium.wasm` and `/data/libdiluvium.wasm` are produced by nothing. This makes the defect worse, not milder: after `make build_static_libs` (:256, which runs both the wasi and the native archive targets), `/data/onelua.o` and `/data/wasm_stubs.o` exist as **native x86 ELF** objects, and llvm-objdump/llvm-nm read ELF happily. Step 1 then finds `setjmp` in the *native* build and step 2 finds `system` (src/wasm_stubs.c:11 `int system(const char *c) { return -1; }`) in the *native* stub — so the target passes while asserting nothing whatsoever about the wasm build. The accurate charge is "inspects paths the wasm build does not produce", not "paths nothing produces".

(2) The failure scenario "The target as a whole then dies at :212" is state-dependent, so state it. After `make build_wasm` (the CI sequence) `.data` holds only `_wasi` names, so :209 passes vacuously and :212 aborts. After `make build_static_libs` the native leftovers make :209 and :212 pass on the wrong objects and :215-216 pass vacuously, so `make verify_wasm` exits 0 having verified nothing. Either way no artifact the wasm build produced is ever inspected.

The proposed fix is right; add that fixing only the filenames while leaving `| head -n 5` in place would leave steps 1 and 3 still unfailable, so both halves must be done together, and the `_wasi` build must run first for the paths to exist (verify_wasm declares no dependency on `build_wasm`).

<a id="f30"></a>

### 30. patch_series.sh check exits 0 having checked nothing when the fork-point commit is unreachable

`script/patch_series.sh`:131 &middot; worth-recording &middot; test-passes-for-wrong-reason &middot; audit `single-point`

**Status: fixed.** `require_fork_point` refuses with exit 2 when the fork point is unreachable, and `check` refuses to print OK for zero modified files. Verified both ways.

**Evidence.**

script/patch_series.sh:63-65 `upstream_files() { git ls-tree --name-only "$FORK_POINT" | grep -E '\.(c|h)$'; }` drives both loops of the `check` command (:131 `for f in $(upstream_files); do` and the count at :153). `set -eu` is in force (:28), but errexit does not fire on a failed command substitution in a `for` word list — verified in this environment: `sh -c 'set -eu; f() { git ls-tree --name-only 000...0 | grep -E "\.(c|h)$"; }; for x in $(f); do echo BODY; done; echo REACHED_END'` prints `fatal: not a tree object` then `REACHED_END`, with no BODY. The stale-allowlist loop at :146-151 cannot compensate: `upstream_copy` (:76-81) runs `git show "$FORK_POINT:$1"` inside a command substitution, which exits nonzero under set -e before its `printf`, so `$(upstream_copy "$f")` is empty, `cmp -s "" "src/$1"` fails, and file_status returns "modified" for every file.

**Failure scenario.**

`git clone --depth 1` (or any clone where 7579fc9d is not fetched) followed by `./script/patch_series.sh check` prints `OK: 0 core files modified, all allowlisted` and exits 0. A developer editing an unallowlisted core file — say src/ldo.c — sees the core patch guard pass locally and only learns otherwise from the patch-guard job, which is safe purely because test.yml:318 sets `fetch-depth: 0`. The script's own header (:9-10) claims the fork point is "permanently available in this repository's own history, so no network access or vendored tarball is needed", which is true only for full clones.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Add an explicit precondition before the loops: `git cat-file -e "$FORK_POINT^{commit}" 2>/dev/null || { echo "fork point $FORK_POINT unreachable (shallow clone?); refusing to check nothing" >&2; exit 2; }`, and assert `[ "$n" -gt 0 ]` before printing OK.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=likely.**

I could not refute it. Every quote is accurate at the cited line, and I reproduced the exact failure rather than taking the reporter's word.

Quote verification: /home/user/diluvium/script/patch_series.sh:28 is `set -eu`; :63-65 is `upstream_files() { git ls-tree --name-only "$FORK_POINT" | grep -E '\.(c|h)$'; }`; :76-81 is `upstream_copy` with `git show "$FORK_POINT:$1" > "$TMPDIR_UP/$1"`; :131 is `for f in $(upstream_files); do`; :146-151 is the stale-allowlist loop; :153 is the `n=$(for f in $(upstream_files); ...)` count. Lines 9-10 read verbatim "branched from -- which is permanently available in this repository's own / history, so no network access or vendored tarball is needed."

Mechanism reproduced: /bin/sh here is dash. `sh -c 'set -eu; f() { git ls-tree --name-only 000...0 | grep -E "\.(c|h)$"; }; for x in $(f); do echo BODY; done; echo REACHED_END'` prints `fatal: not a tree object` then `REACHED_END` with exit 0 — errexit does not fire on a failed command substitution in a `for` word list. I then replicated lines 63-92 plus the whole `check` block inline against this tree with an unreachable FORK_POINT: output was no NOT ALLOWED, no MISSING, no STALE ALLOWLIST ENTRY, then `OK: 0 core files modified, all allowlisted`, exit 0. The stale loop cannot compensate exactly as described: `$(file_status "$f")` sits inside an `if` condition where errexit is suppressed, `upstream_copy` yields either an empty path or a zero-byte temp file, `cmp -s` fails, and every allowlisted file reports `modified` rather than `pristine`.

Trigger is reachable: 7579fc9d is 123 commits behind HEAD (`git rev-list --count 7579fc9d..HEAD` = 123), so `git clone --depth 1` leaves it absent. A build and the test suite still work in that clone; only this guard silently degrades.

Nothing compensates: no reference to patch_series in test/, script/, bindings/, or any Makefile; the only `git cat-file -e` in script/ is at :125 inside `list`, not a precondition; and no README/doc anywhere states a full-clone requirement (`grep -rniE "git clone|shallow|fetch-depth" --include=*.md .` returns nothing).

Refutations I tried and rejected: (1) "CI protects it" — true, .github/workflows/test.yml:318 is `fetch-depth: 0   # the fork-point commit must be reachable` and all four workflows use depth 0, but the finding already discloses this and scopes itself to local runs; doc/Analyzer.md:113-114 says the determinism verdict is "worth confirming with `patch_series.sh check`", i.e. developers are told to run it locally, so a vacuous OK is a genuine false-confirmation path. (2) "bash would catch it" — errexit does not fire in a `for` word list in bash either, and the shebang is /bin/sh = dash regardless. (3) "the doc states the limitation nearby" — doc/Messaging.md section 3.1 (the only place it discusses the patch policy, around :97) says nothing about clone depth, and neither does doc/ROADMAP.md:438.

Confidence is "likely" rather than "certain" only on severity, not on facts: the guard's false `OK: 0 core files modified, all allowlisted` is demonstrated, but the sole trigger is a non-default local clone and CI is explicitly immunised, so it sits at the reporter's own "worth-recording" level, not a shipped defect.

*Correction to the report:* Two refinements to the report as written. (1) Drop the lines 9-10 header quote as evidence — it is the weakest part and I would not defend it. The fork-point commit genuinely is permanent in this repository's history; a shallow clone is a truncated local copy, so the sentence is not itself a false statement. The finding stands on the demonstrated false output `OK: 0 core files modified, all allowlisted` alone. (2) Add the supporting evidence the reporter missed: doc/Analyzer.md:113-114 ("The determinism verdict adds nothing to the 14 files in `script/patch_series.sh` -- worth confirming with `patch_series.sh check` when the time coes") directs developers to run the guard locally, and nothing in test/, script/, bindings/ or any Makefile exercises the script, so the vacuous-OK path has no compensating assertion outside the CI `fetch-depth: 0` line at .github/workflows/test.yml:318.

<a id="f31"></a>

### 31. The include_skipped input description names three tests that are no longer skipped, and understates what the toggle does

`.github/workflows/test.yml`:22 &middot; worth-recording &middot; false-doc-claim &middot; audit `single-point`

**Status: fixed.** Checked against `run_tests.sh --list-skipped`: three tests, not six. The description names them, says guards still skip under the toggle, and the `workflow_call` input has a description at all.

**Evidence.**

test.yml:22 verbatim: `description: 'Also run the normally-skipped tests (api, attrib, big, heavy, literals, main)'`. `./test/run_tests.sh --list-skipped` now reports exactly three: big, heavy, main. api (run_tests.sh:69), attrib (:70) and literals (:89) are all `run`. Furthermore run_tests.sh:237-243 evaluates guards even under --include-skipped ("A guard is a precondition evaluated now, so it applies even to an explicitly named test and even with --include-skipped"), so setting the toggle cannot make attrib or literals run where their guards fire.

**Failure scenario.**

An operator dispatching Tests with include_skipped=true to answer "does attrib pass on macOS?" reads this description as a promise that it will run, gets a green run in which attrib and literals were both silently skipped by their guards, and concludes the answer is yes. The stale list also mis-states the project's own headline claim about which tests were recovered.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Change test.yml:22 (and the identical shape at test.yml:32-35's workflow_call input) to name only big, heavy and main, and note that guarded tests still skip when their precondition is absent.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=likely.**

Both quotes check out at the claimed lines, and the inconsistency is real, but three of the report's supporting claims are wrong and its scenario is overstated.

VERIFIED
- .github/workflows/test.yml:22 is verbatim: `        description: 'Also run the normally-skipped tests (api, attrib, big, heavy, literals, main)'` — the only `description:` on either `include_skipped` input.
- test/run_tests.sh table (67-119): only three rows carry `skip` — `big|skip` (:88 is heavy, :72 is big), `heavy|skip` (:88), `main|skip` (:91). `api|run` (:69, no guard), `attrib|run` (:70, guard_no_test_libs), `literals|run` (:89, guard_not_glibc). So `--list-skipped` (which filters on `[ "$status" = skip ]`, :161) prints exactly big, heavy, main.
- The toggle provably cannot affect the three stale names. `--include-skipped` only sets INCLUDE_SKIPPED (:142), consumed at :231 for `status = skip` rows alone. api has no guard and runs on every platform every run. attrib/literals are gated at :239 by guards that :237-238 documents apply "even with --include-skipped". So for three of the six names the description's promise ("Also run") is false, and for api the premise ("normally-skipped") is false on every platform.
- Drift confirmed by history: the string entered in 9cefbf7 (2026-08-04); the table moved api/attrib/literals to `run` in c39649c (2026-08-10), which is the same commit whose doc/Messaging.md lesson (2595-2608) is "A stated reason goes stale silently; an evaluated one cannot." test.yml was not touched by it (its last touch is b7db757).

REFUTED / CORRECTED (why I did not accept the report as written)
1. "silently skipped" is false. run_tests.sh:240 prints `SKIP  %-18s %s` with the reason, :279 prints `passed N failed M skipped K`, and :284-296 writes a skipped count into GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY. An operator asking "does attrib pass on macOS?" reads a log containing `SKIP  attrib  needs the test C libraries: run 'make test_libs'`. The step summary omits the names, so the toggle can still mislead, but not silently.
2. The title's "three tests that are no longer skipped" is only true on the Linux leg. Makefile:262 is `test_build: _build_step0 test_libs`, so CI's `make test_build` builds test/libs/*.so, and Makefile:54 links the debug binary with `-Wl,-E`, so lib1.so dlopens and attrib RUNS on ubuntu-latest. On macOS PLATFORM_CFLAGS drops to `-DLUA_USE_POSIX` (Makefile:57) and the libs' `gcc -shared` has no Lua to link against, so attrib is skipped there every run, and literals is skipped there every run (no ldd/glibc). Calling attrib and literals "normally-skipped" is therefore accurate on the macOS leg; the unambiguously stale name is `api`.
3. The proposed fix's "the identical shape at test.yml:32-35's workflow_call input" does not exist — lines 32-35 are `type`, `required`, `default` only, with no description. Line 22 is the sole occurrence.
4. Nothing in doc/Messaging.md is contradicted; 2298-2301 and 2595-2608 record the recovery honestly ("47 tests run now instead of 44"). The defect is confined to the workflow's dispatch-form string, and there is no wrong result or crash — severity is documentation drift, which matches the reporter's own "worth-recording".

*Correction to the report:* Keep the finding, narrowed: .github/workflows/test.yml:22 states the toggle will "Also run the normally-skipped tests (api, attrib, big, heavy, literals, main)", but test/run_tests.sh marks only big (:72), heavy (:88) and main (:91) as `skip`, and `--include-skipped` reaches nothing else — api (:69) has no guard and runs on every platform, while attrib (:70) and literals (:89) are gated by guards that :237-239 documents as applying "even with --include-skipped". Drop the claim that the skips are "silent" (:240 prints `SKIP <name> <reason>`, :279 a skipped count, :284-296 a job-summary count) and drop the claim that all three "are no longer skipped": on ubuntu-latest all three run (Makefile:262 makes test_build build test_libs; Makefile:54 links with -Wl,-E so lib1.so loads), but on macos-latest attrib and literals are still skipped every run, so the honest fix is to say the flag reaches only big, heavy and main and that guarded tests skip whenever their precondition is absent. Also drop the fix's reference to test.yml:32-35 — that workflow_call input has no description field.

<a id="f32"></a>

### 32. "A stray lua.h fails the build" is not a check that exists; dvs.c already includes lua.h

`doc/Messaging.md`:2158 &middot; worth-recording &middot; false-doc-claim &middot; audit `doc-claims`

**Status: fixed.** Same as #3.

**Evidence.**

doc:2157-2159: '...the Makefile compiles it apart from the amalgamation so a stray `lua.h` fails the build rather than passing unnoticed.' src/dvs.c:26 is `#include "dmsgpack.h"` and src/dmsgpack.h:22 is an unconditional `#include "lua.h"`, so lua.h is already in dvs.c's translation unit. Both compile sites pass -I .data, which holds a copy of lua.h (.data/lua.h exists), and dist/libdiluvium-swarm.a was produced at 17:11 today, so the build is green with lua.h in scope. The guard that actually exists is link-level: Makefile:415-421 runs `nm -u dist/dvs.o | ... grep -E '^_?lua'` and fails the target if any core symbol is referenced.

**Failure scenario.**

Someone adds `#include "lua.h"` and a `lua_State *` member to dvs.c. Nothing fails: the preprocessor finds lua.h, and dvs_check (Makefile:319-324) links onelua.c so even a real lua_* call resolves there. Only build_swarm_lib would catch a symbol reference, and only if that target is run. A reader trusting this sentence believes the boundary is enforced one layer earlier than it is.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

State the guarantee the tree actually has (the nm -u check in build_swarm_lib, link-time, on symbols not includes). If a preprocessor-level boundary is wanted, split the token-cursor declarations out of dmsgpack.h into a header that does not include lua.h, and then the sentence becomes true.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=certain.**

I tried to refute this and could not; every load-bearing quote checks out at the stated line, and no reading of the doc sentence survives.

Doc, verbatim, /home/user/diluvium/doc/Messaging.md:2156-2160:
"It includes `dv.h` and the / codec's token cursor and nothing else from the tree; the Makefile compiles it apart / from the amalgamation so a stray `lua.h` fails the build rather than passing / unnoticed. That is 4.1's boundary made checkable instead of merely stated: the / moment this file needs a `lua_State`, something has been put in the wrong layer."

Quotes confirmed at the exact lines:
- /home/user/diluvium/src/dvs.c:26 is `#include "dmsgpack.h"` (line 27 is `#include "dvs.h"`).
- /home/user/diluvium/src/dmsgpack.h:22 is `#include "lua.h"`, unconditional — the only preprocessor conditionals in that file are the `#ifndef dmsgpack_h` guard at :16 and its `#endif` at :383, so nothing gates it. It is there because the cursor prototypes are declared `LUA_API` (dmsgpack.h:128,137,140,148), a luaconf.h macro — and dmsgpack.h:82 even says "A read-only token cursor, with no 'lua_State' anywhere in sight."
- /home/user/diluvium/src/dv.h includes only <stddef.h> and <stdint.h> (:34-35), so dvs.h's own narrower claim (dvs.h:32) is clean; the leak is via dmsgpack.h into dvs.c.
- Both compile sites pass `-I$(CURDIR)/.data`: Makefile:320 (dvs_check) and Makefile:413 (build_swarm_lib), and Makefile:65-70 `_build_step0` does `rm -rf .data/*` then `cp -r src/* .data`, so .data/lua.h (16723 bytes) is on the include path by construction.

So lua.h is already in dvs.c's translation unit today. A "stray `lua.h`" cannot fail the build; it is a no-op re-include of a header the file already gets, and even a `lua_State *` member — the exact thing the following sentence says would be caught — compiles cleanly, since lua.h supplies `typedef struct lua_State lua_State;`. This is not a hypothetical: dist/dvs.o and dist/libdiluvium-swarm.a exist (23392 / 23834 bytes) and dist/dvs_check exists, i.e. both targets have been compiled green with lua.h in scope.

The only guard that exists is at symbol level, Makefile:415-421: `nm -u dist/dvs.o | awk '{print $NF}' | grep -E '^_?lua'` with an `exit 1`. That catches a *reference to a lua_ symbol*, not an include and not a `lua_State` field. Confirmed dvs.c references none (the sole `lua_` hit in the file is the comment at dvs.c:7).

Refutation attempts that failed:
- No textual/preprocessor guard exists anywhere: grepping `lua\.h` across script/, test/, bindings/ and .github/ finds only script/changelog.py (version strings in src/lua.h), test/libs/makefile deps, upstream copyright headers, and test TUs that include it deliberately (test/dvs_check.c:32, test/dsnap_check.c:67, test/mp_cursor_fuzz.c:37). Nothing inspects dvs.c's includes.
- Reading it as "a stray core dependency fails the build" doesn't rescue it either: dvs_check (Makefile:319-324) links .data/onelua.c, so even a real lua_* call resolves at link time in that target.
- The Makefile comment at :316-318 repeats the same wrong claim ("a stray include of lua.h would fail here rather than be absorbed by the amalgamation"), so the doc is not misquoting the tree — the tree is wrong in both places.
- I read the whole M7 block (doc:2145-2180): nothing later in the section states the limitation or names the nm check. Grepping the doc for "nm -u", "undefined symbol", "link-time"/"link time", "libdiluvium-swarm" returns only doc:174, doc:1592 and doc:2155, none of which mention the actual guard.

Two corrections to the reporter's evidence, neither material: dist/libdiluvium-swarm.a is timestamped 17:35, not 17:11; and build_swarm_lib is not merely optional — .github/workflows/test.yml:186 runs `make build_swarm_lib` (and :156 runs `make dvs_check`), so the nm check does run in CI. That makes the failure scenario's "only if that target is run" overstated, but it does not save the sentence: the nm check passes a `lua_State *` member or any lua.h include, so the boundary the doc calls "made checkable" is checked one layer later and on a different property than the doc states.

*Correction to the report:* Keep the finding, with the failure scenario narrowed. The doc sentence at doc/Messaging.md:2157-2159 is false as written: lua.h is already in dvs.c's TU via src/dvs.c:26 -> src/dmsgpack.h:22 (unconditional `#include "lua.h"`, needed for the `LUA_API` on the cursor prototypes), and both compile sites pass -I .data where _build_step0 (Makefile:65-70) has copied src/lua.h. dist/dvs.o and dist/libdiluvium-swarm.a are proof the build is green in that state. The same wrong claim is in the Makefile comment at :316-318 and should be fixed with it. Drop the reporter's "only if that target is run" caveat: .github/workflows/test.yml:186 does run `make build_swarm_lib`, so the real guarantee is a link-level one that fires in CI — `nm -u dist/dvs.o ... grep -E '^_?lua'` at Makefile:415-421 — which catches a reference to a core symbol but not an include of lua.h and not a `lua_State *` member, so doc:2160's "the moment this file needs a `lua_State`, something has been put in the wrong layer" is still unenforced. Also note the timestamp is 17:35, not 17:11. The proposed fix (state the nm check as the actual guarantee; split the cursor declarations into a header free of lua.h if a preprocessor-level boundary is wanted) is sound.

<a id="f33"></a>

### 33. do_query emits an event named 'gone', which 9.2 says is not one of the events

`/home/user/diluvium/src/dvs.c`:1023 &middot; worth-recording &middot; false-doc-claim &middot; audit `hostile-guest`

**Status: fixed.** Same as #28.

**Evidence.**

doc/Messaging.md:896: "The events are `spawned`, `exited`, `faulted`, `exceeded`, `hibernated`, `throttled`, `denied`, and `status`." The list is stated exhaustively and `gone` is not in it -- in the doc `"gone"` is a queue/endpoint *status* (6.4, doc:467), a different vocabulary. The code:

  sl = find(sw, (dvs_id)target);
  if (sl == NULL) {
    emit_event(sw, parent->id, "gone", (dvs_id)target, NULL);   /* dvs.c:1023 */

Measured (test/dvs_attack2.c case 2, a query for id 77 in an eight-slot swarm holding only the root):

    === 2. query for an id that does not exist
        [root] event=gone id=77 detail=

Every other malformed-request path answers `denied`, so a supervisor written against 9.2 -- e.g. dvs_check.c's own `if e.event == 'exited' or e.event == 'faulted' or e.event == 'exceeded'` dispatch -- has no branch for this one and silently ignores it.

**Failure scenario.**

A supervisor queries a child it believes may have died, in order to decide whether to restart it. The child is already reaped, so the swarm answers `{event = 'gone', id = N}`. The supervisor's dispatch, written from 9.2's list, matches none of its cases, so it neither restarts nor escalates and waits forever for an event that will never come. Reusing `gone`, which 6.4 defines as a push status, also makes a program that funnels both through one string comparison mis-handle one of them.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Emit `denied` with a "no such instance" detail (matching do_kill at dvs.c:935), or add `gone` to 9.2's list and say what a supervisor should do with it. The list is written as exhaustive, so one of the two has to move.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=certain.**

Both quotes verified verbatim at the stated locations, and the doc sentence is false as written.

CODE: /home/user/diluvium/src/dvs.c:1023 is exactly `emit_event(sw, parent->id, "gone", (dvs_id)target, NULL);`, inside `do_query`, guarded by `sl = find(sw, (dvs_id)target); if (sl == NULL) {`. `emit_event` (src/dvs.c:502-551) writes that string as the value of the `event` key of a message pushed to the subject's `system/events` queue — the same field and channel 9.2 enumerates.

DOC: doc/Messaging.md:896-897 reads verbatim "The events are `spawned`, `exited`, `faulted`, `exceeded`, `hibernated`, `throttled`,\n`denied`, and `status`." I read the whole of 9.2 (871-900), 9.3-9.5 including the "Two corrections from building it" block (941-968), and the M7 milestone block (2147-2200). None names `gone` as an event. All 17 doc occurrences of "gone" are the push/endpoint status vocabulary (467 is 6.4's status table, plus 543, 566, 937, 939, 1866, 1910, 1912, 1928, 2316). The next sentence after the list ("`spawned` and `denied` are additions the draft's list implies but does not state") shows the author treating the list as a maintained inventory, so the exhaustive reading is the intended one. There is no later sentence in the section that rescues it.

REACHABLE: `drain` dispatches `else if (strcmp(op, "query") == 0) do_query(sw, sl, (const char *)buf, n);` at src/dvs.c:1095-1096, gated only by `dvs_holds(sw, sl->id, DVS_CAP_LIFECYCLE)` at src/dvs.c:1043. `query` is one of the four ops the doc itself lists for `system/lifecycle` (doc:875). Any supervisor pushing {op='query', id=N} for an N not in the slot table lands on line 1023.

NO EARLIER CHECK PREVENTS IT — and the scenario is stronger than reported. `find` matches `slots[i].id == id`; `release` sets `sl->alive = 0; sl->id = 0;` at src/dvs.c:205-206; `dvs_step` calls `kill_subtree(sw, id, 0)` the instant an instance stops (src/dvs.c:1201) and on a failed wake (src/dvs.c:1149). So a child that exited, faulted, or exceeded is unfindable in the same step, and `gone` is the answer to the ordinary post-mortem query a supervisor makes, not a corner case. (Side effect: the `sl->alive ? "alive" : "dead"` detail at src/dvs.c:1027 can essentially never print "dead", since the only writer of alive=0 also clears id.) The claim "every other malformed-request path answers denied" holds: do_kill answers `"denied", target, "no such instance"` at src/dvs.c:935 and do_hibernate the same at src/dvs.c:972, so the identical condition produces two different event names depending on the op.

UNCHECKED CONFIRMED BY SEARCHING FOR THE BEHAVIOUR: test/dvs_check.c has no test of the query op at all (its only "query" hit, line 873, is a comment about a host capability query); `grep -rn gone test/` returns only push/endpoint-status assertions (dvs_check.c:714/795/811/814, dv_check.c:570-573, test_endpoint.lua:90); bindings/ and script/ contain no event-name strings. The supervisor dispatch quoted from the report is real at test/dvs_check.c:249: "  if e.event == 'exited' or e.event == 'faulted' or e.event == 'exceeded' then\n".

This satisfies criterion (b) independently of the prose scenario: a sentence in doc/Messaging.md quoted verbatim that the code contradicts, in the document that is also the milestone record.

*Correction to the report:* Two refinements the project should record with the finding.

(1) The cited evidence file is not in the repo. `test/dvs_attack2.c` does not exist in /home/user/diluvium/test/; it is the reporting agent's scratch harness in a sibling worktree (/home/user/diluvium/.claude/worktrees/wf_ddad3a8f-479-10/test/dvs_attack2.c, "== 2. 'gone' is not one of 9.2's events" at line 164, pushing `{op = 'query', id = 77}` at line 171). I verified that worktree's src/dvs.c is byte-identical to the checkout's (diff -q reports identical), so the measurement is against this code — but it must be cited as a scratch harness, not as a repo test. The repo has no test of the query op whatsoever, which is why this vocabulary was never validated.

(2) The failure scenario should be stated more strongly. The report frames `gone` as the answer when "the child is already reaped", implying reaping is incidental. It is immediate and unconditional: dvs_step calls kill_subtree the same step an instance stops (src/dvs.c:1201), and release clears the id (src/dvs.c:206). So `gone` is the answer to every query about a child that has died — the most natural query a supervisor issues — and `status ... dead` is effectively unreachable. Correspondingly the proposed fix should note the code's own inconsistency: do_kill (src/dvs.c:935) and do_hibernate (src/dvs.c:972) already answer `denied` with "no such instance" for the identical condition, so making do_query match is the smaller change and leaves 9.2's list true.

---

<a id="f34"></a>

### 34. kill_subtree recurses once per chain level, contrary to its own comment, and a deep chain overflows the C stack

`/home/user/diluvium/src/dvs.c`:578 &middot; worth-recording &middot; robustness &middot; audit `hostile-guest`

**Status: fixed.** Same as #20, including the overstatement.

**Evidence.**

dvs.c:568-573 states the hazard and claims it was avoided: "Children first, and by repeated scan rather than a recursive walk: the table is flat, a subtree can be as deep as delegation went, and 9.1.1 says depth is unbounded. A recursive walk would meet the C stack at a depth a program is entitled to reach." The loop below it iterates over *siblings* but recurses on depth:

  for (;;) {
    int found = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < sw->nslots; i++) {
      if (sw->slots[i].id != 0 && sw->slots[i].parent == id) {
        kill_subtree(sw, sw->slots[i].id, 0);        /* dvs.c:578 */

A self-reproducing agent (test/dvs_attack3.c --deep N) builds a parent chain N deep, then dvs_kill on the root. Under the sanitizer build with a 128 KiB stack and a 2000-deep chain:

    $ bash -c 'ulimit -s 128; ./dist/dvs_attack3 --deep 2000'
        deepest chain=2000
        dvs_kill(root) ...
    ... #246 0x... in kill_subtree .data/dvs.c:578   [246 identical frames]
    SUMMARY: AddressSanitizer: stack-overflow .data/dvs.c:147 in find

and at -O2 without sanitizers, `ulimit -s 128` plus a 3000-deep chain gives "Segmentation fault" (rc 139) after "dvs_kill(root) ...", while the same chain survives at `ulimit -s 256` -- so the frame is roughly 45-85 bytes. At 3000 deep on a default 8 MiB stack it completes cleanly (alive=0), so the depth needed there is order 10^5 instances, which memory makes impractical for me to reach.

**Failure scenario.**

A host that drives the swarm from a thread with a small stack rather than the main thread -- 128 KiB is what I measured against, and the doc's target list includes wasm32 where small linear stacks are normal -- plus a guest holding `lifecycle` that spawns one child per generation. At 3000 live instances, one dvs_kill on the root (or a supervisor exiting, since dvs_step's drive loop calls kill_subtree at dvs.c:1201) segfaults the host process. The guest needs no capability it was not granted: 9.1.1 makes the chain the ordinary shape of recursive delegation.

**Proposed fix, as filed.**

Make the walk genuinely iterative, as the comment already says it should be: mark the subtree with an explicit worklist (the slot array can hold the stack, since a slot is either in the subtree or not), or repeatedly scan for a slot whose id is in the marked set and has no live children, releasing it, until none remain. Either way the C stack depth becomes constant. At minimum, correct the comment so the next reader does not take the property on trust.

**Verdict: real=True, confidence=likely.**

Both quotes check out at the stated lines. /home/user/diluvium/src/dvs.c:568-573 says verbatim "Children first, and by repeated scan rather than a recursive walk: the table is flat, a subtree can be as deep as delegation went, and 9.1.1 says depth is unbounded. A recursive walk would meet the C stack at a depth a program is entitled to reach." and dvs.c:578 is exactly "        kill_subtree(sw, sw->slots[i].id, 0);". The repeated scan handles siblings only; recursion depth equals subtree depth, so the comment states a stack-safety property the code does not have. That falsehood is demonstrable by reading alone, at an exact line.

Reachability traced: dvs_kill -> kill_subtree (dvs.c:596), the lifecycle 'kill' op (dvs.c:945), and the drive loop (dvs.c:1149, 1201). No depth cap exists (grep "depth" in dvs.c hits only the comment). Deep chains need only the 'lifecycle' capability, which 9.1.1 says is the ordinary shape of delegation; the reporter's reproducer really exists at .claude/worktrees/wf_ddad3a8f-479-10/test/dvs_attack3.c:252-292 and prints "deepest chain=2000". Recursion cannot loop: ids are monotonic (dvs.c:341 "sw->slots[i].id = sw->next_id++") and never reused (dvs.c:206), so a parent id is always below its child's.

Attempts to refute that failed: no earlier check blocks a deep chain; the only in-tree test of subtree depth is three generations with a 4-slot table (test/dvs_check.c:460-500), so nothing pins the property; and dvs.h:155 / doc 9.5 make no promise that would make the crash out of contract.

The finding does overstate its scope, and the correction field records that: depth is bounded by sw->nslots = max_instances (default DVS_DEFAULT_MAX 256, dvs.c:31, documented dvs.h:99), no doc/Messaging.md sentence is contradicted (9.1.1 line 837's "depth is unbounded" is a model statement, and the table bound is documented separately), and the measured segfault needs a 128 KiB stack that nothing in this tree sets, or ~10^5 live instances on a default stack, which the reporter concedes is out of reach. Marked real because the unconditional part - the source asserting a property it does not have, at a named line, with a crash that does follow once depth exceeds the frame budget - is verified rather than "this looks risky", and the reporter bounded their own claim honestly.

*Correction to the report:* Keep the finding, but record it as what it is: a false claim in the source plus a configuration-dependent robustness limit, not an unbounded-depth crash and not a doc contradiction.

1. The durable defect is the comment. dvs.c:568-573 claims the walk is "by repeated scan rather than a recursive walk" and that "A recursive walk would meet the C stack at a depth a program is entitled to reach", directly above the recursive call at dvs.c:578. The scan is over siblings; depth is recursive. Either make the walk iterative or correct the comment - leaving it is exactly a property taken on trust.

2. Depth is not unbounded. It is bounded by sw->nslots, i.e. the host's max_instances argument to dvs_new, default DVS_DEFAULT_MAX 256 (dvs.c:31; dvs.h:99 "'max_instances' bounds the table"). At the default that is ~256 frames of a ~48-64 byte frame - nowhere near any plausible stack. The reporter's 2000/3000-deep runs required the host to configure max_instances >= 3000 (their swarm_with(MAX, MAX)).

3. No sentence in doc/Messaging.md is contradicted. 9.1.1 line 837 - "Parentage is a single field per instance, depth is unbounded, and subtree kill means what it says." - is a statement about the delegation model, and the table bound is documented in dvs.h; neither the doc nor dvs.h:155 ever claims the kill is iterative or stack-safe. Drop the implication that the doc is wrong.

4. Scope the crash honestly: the demonstrated segfault needs a stack ~64x smaller than the glibc default (the reporter's ulimit -s 128) together with a 3000-slot table; on a default 8 MiB stack it needs order 10^5 live instances, which the reporter states they could not reach. That is a real limit for a small-stack driving thread or a wasm32 build with a reduced linear stack, and not reachable at the default table size.

---

## Found since the sweep

Two defects that the ten audits did not find, recorded here because this file is
where someone looks. **They are not part of the 35/32 counts above**, which belong
to the sweep of 2026-08-10 and should stay as that run left them.

Both were found the same way, and it is worth naming: by taking a sentence that was
believed true and checking it against a running binary rather than against the
source. The first came out of implementing finding 6; the second out of writing the
release note that claimed finding 6 was the last thing in the way.

### S0. `debug.sethook()` switches off the instruction budget

`src/dv.c` &middot; blocks-building-on-it &middot; correctness

**Status: fixed** by the same change as finding 6 -- `debug.sethook` is one of the
twelve refusals. `a_guest_cannot_switch_its_own_budget_off` (`dv_check`).

A `lua_State` has one hook slot. §9.4's instruction budget is a count hook armed on
the instance's thread in `dv_run`, and `debug.sethook()` with no arguments is the
documented way to clear a hook. Measured before the fix: an instance given 200,000
instructions ran a three-million-iteration loop to completion, `dv_run` returned
`DV_DONE`, `dv_exceeded` was 0 and `dv_usage` reported `insn_used` of 0.

One line, no setup, no authorised peer, no capability. It is the same shape as
findings 2 and 7 -- a budget stored, reported by an accessor, and enforced by
nothing -- reached from the guest side rather than the host side, and it is the more
serious of the two routes finding 6's fix closed.

`doc/Lab.md` records the host-side half of this ("a debug hook installed naively
replaces the budget hook and silently disables the budget") and did not draw the
guest-side conclusion. Worth noting as a near miss: the fact was written down and
the consequence was not.

### S1. An instance reaches `os.execute`, `io.popen` and `package.loadlib`

`src/dv.c` &middot; blocks-building-on-it &middot; false-doc-claim

**Status: fixed.** An instance is sealed by default -- no `io`, `os` or `package`, and
no `dofile`/`loadfile` -- and `DV_FLAG_UNSAFE_STDLIB` undoes it for programs that
predate the default. `a_sealed_instance_reaches_nothing_outside_itself` and
`an_instance_is_sealed_by_default` (`dv_check`), plus
`flags_attenuate_through_a_spawn` (`dvs_check`), which covers a second defect found
with it: `build` zeroed its `dv_config` and never read the parent's, so a sealed
supervisor spawned children that had `os.execute`. Flags were the one authority in the
swarm layer that did not narrow.

`dv_new` called `luaL_openlibs`, so an instance got every standard library.
Measured: `os.execute`, `os.remove`, `io.popen`, `io.open`, `package.loadlib`,
`require`, `dofile` and `loadfile` are all present, and `io.open('/etc/passwd')`
returns a file handle.

Why it matters more than its obviousness suggests: §18.2's profile B -- "untrusted
or generated programs" -- named the `debug` library as the one item between a
deployment and running code it did not write. That list was incomplete, and the
incompleteness pointed the wrong way, because a program that can start a process
does not need to forge an endpoint reference. Narrowing `debug` makes the
*capability layer* a boundary. It does not make the *instance* one, and the two had
been conflated.

`doc/Messaging.md` does not discuss the standard library surface anywhere -- §8.5
fixes the signature of a permission check and calls the token model separate work,
and nothing says which libraries a guest gets. So this was never decided, only
inherited from `luaL_openlibs`.

The default was the open question, and it is settled: sealed. The deciding argument was
not security but arithmetic -- `dv_new` has shipped exactly once, in a prerelease that
is neither `latest` nor mirrored, so the population writing against the unsafe default
is as small as it will ever be and grows from the moment build4 becomes `latest`.
Folding it into build4 also means `DV_FLAG_SEALED` never ships and is not deprecated a
release after being introduced.

What remains is not a defect but an absence, and it is 18.3's item: a sealed program has
no way to *ask* for the time. `io`/`os`/`package` were a second boundary beside the one
the design has -- yield a request, the host answers -- and removing the second one does
not populate the first. Until a hostcall exists, `DV_FLAG_UNSAFE_STDLIB` is the only
answer for a program that needs a clock, which is what makes it scaffolding rather than
a configuration.

---

## Refuted findings

Kept in full. Each of these was filed by an auditor and then taken apart by a
skeptic; the reasoning below is the skeptic's. Read one before filing anything
similar.

### R0. A guest constructs endpoint references itself: msgpack.decode is the missing constructor

audit `m0-m2`

Stale finding: it describes a vulnerability that was already found, fixed, tested and documented in this tree (commit bbb4509 "Endpoint references could be forged by any guest; close it"). Every load-bearing element of the report fails against current source.

(a) Wrong location and the guard is present. src/dmsgpack.c:1065 is inside mp_decode_array (`lua_rawseti(L, -2, (lua_Integer)i);`), not mp_decode_ext, which starts at line 1087. Its `case 0x02` (line 1103) begins with the exact defence the reporter says is missing, at src/dmsgpack.c:1121: `if (!c->trusted) { mp_ext_push(L, code, data, len); return; }`. `int trusted;` is declared at src/dmsgpack.c:964; it is set to 0 in the guest-callable `mp_decode` at src/dmsgpack.c:1342 (`c.trusted = 0;  /* a guest's own string mints no references */`) and to 1 only in `diluvium_msgpack_decode_n` at src/dmsgpack.c:1383 (queue delivery) and snapshot restore at src/dmsgpack.c:1685. So de_resolve/de_push_ref are unreachable from msgpack.decode.

(b) The repro does not reproduce. dist/diluvium_debug (mtime 17:42) is newer than src/dmsgpack.c (17:33). Running the reporter's own snippet: `msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02Z")` yields a table whose getmetatable is a VISIBLE table (not false) with `r[3] == 2` — the opaque ext wrapper — and `pcall(endpoint.bind, r, "peer")` returns `endpoint.bind: the first argument is not an endpoint reference. A program receives one in a message and never builds one; see 7.3.`, the same message as `pcall(endpoint.bind, {"b"}, "x")`. The claimed distinguishing error "endpoint.bind: this host binds no endpoints" never appears, so the object never passes de_ref_bytes. `msgpack.decode("\xc7\x06\x02peer-b")` is refused identically.

(c) The doc quote is fabricated/stale and the doc says the opposite. doc/Messaging.md:1880 actually reads: `**"A reference cannot be forged" was false when written, and is now true.**` Lines 1883-1897 spell out the very attack the reporter claims to have discovered (including the dv_endpoint_allow pre-authorised-peer demonstration) and the very fix proposed ("only the host's reach the resolver: the queue delivery path and snapshot restore are trusted, guest-callable `msgpack.decode` is not"). Section 17 records it at doc/Messaging.md:2638 as the document's most serious correction. doc/Messaging.md:561's "never constructed by the guest" is therefore true of this tree.

(d) The proposed test already exists. test/test_endpoint.lua:63-65 puts `msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02Z")` and `msgpack.decode("\xc7\x06\x02peer-b")` in the forgery list and asserts both are refused with "never builds one"; lines 42-51 assert the decoded ext has a visible metatable and is rejected by bind; lines 28-41 and 73-77 explain that the earlier assertions which blessed the hole were removed. The reporter's claim that "test/test_endpoint.lua:43 only tries {}, {"bytes"}, setmetatable({}, {}), "string", 5" and that ":31 and :53 obtain a reference by calling msgpack.decode" describes the pre-bbb4509 file.

---

### R1. array32/map32 length prefix pre-allocates before any element is read: 5 bytes of input, 178 MB of RSS

audit `m0-m2`

Already fixed in this tree; the finding is stale and its code quote is fabricated relative to the current source.

1) QUOTE DOES NOT MATCH THE FILE. The finding locates itself at src/dmsgpack.c:1017 and quotes `lua_createtable(L, (int)((n > (size_t)INT_MAX) ? 0 : n), 0);`. Line 1017 is actually `return (lua_Integer)n;`, the last statement of `mp_to_integer`. The quoted string does not occur anywhere in src/dmsgpack.c (grep for `lua_createtable` returns lines 484, 517, 530, 553, 568, 1053, 1069 only). It is the pre-fix text of what is now line 1053.

2) THE PROPOSED FIX IS ALREADY IMPLEMENTED, VERBATIM IN SPIRIT. src/dmsgpack.c:1042-1046:
   static int mp_size_hint (const mp_cur *c, size_t n) {
     if (n > c->left)
       n = c->left;
     return (int)((n > (size_t)INT_MAX) ? 0 : n);
   }
   src/dmsgpack.c:1053 (mp_decode_array): `lua_createtable(L, mp_size_hint(c, n), 0);`
   src/dmsgpack.c:1069 (mp_decode_map): `lua_createtable(L, 0, mp_size_hint(c, (n > c->left / 2) ? c->left / 2 : n));`
   This is exactly the reporter's "pass `n <= c->left ? (int)n : 0`", with the INT_MAX guard kept, plus the halving for map pairs the reporter also asked for. A 40-line comment at 1024-1041 records the same measurement (131 MB from `dd 08 00 00 00`) and the same store-and-forward threat model.

3) THE CLAMP CANNOT BE SIDESTEPPED. Every assignment to the cursor's `left` derives from a real buffer length: src/dmsgpack.c:1340 (`c.left = len - (size_t)(offset - 1)`), 1379, 1678, 1888 (`c->left = len`). There is no path where `left` is a sentinel or unbounded value, so `min(claim, left)` bounds the pre-size by the actual input size on every entry point, including the queue-delivery decode the failure scenario relies on.

4) FIXED AS ITS OWN COMMIT WITH A REMOVAL-VERIFIED TEST. `git log` shows 4f52942 "A 5-byte message could make the decoder allocate 131 MB; and dv_load's NULL guard could never fire", touching src/dmsgpack.c (+31) and test/mp_cursor_fuzz.c (+88). The added test builds a bomb table containing precisely the case the reporter says is missing: `{ "array32 claiming 134M elements", { (char)0xdd, 0x08, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00 }, 5 }`, plus the 2^31-1 array32, `0xdf` map32, and array16 65535 cases; it decodes each under a counting allocator and fails if `cnt.peak > baseline + 1024 * 1024`. The commit message states the mutation check was performed and records the meta-lesson that without the clamp the target aborts under ASan OOM rather than printing `[FAIL]` — i.e. convention 2 was satisfied.

5) THE REPORTER'S "NO TEST" CLAIM SEARCHED THE WRONG FILE. It asserts test/test_msgpack.lua never covers an oversized length prefix. True, but irrelevant: the assertion is deliberately in C in test/mp_cursor_fuzz.c through a counting allocator, because the commit explains an RSS-based bound would be allocator-dependent and flaky. Searching for the behaviour rather than the file finds it.

6) THE MEASUREMENTS ARE AGAINST A STALE BINARY. The RSS figures (178,452 kB / 199,208 kB) were taken from dist/diluvium_debug. src/dmsgpack.c has mtime 2026-08-10T17:33 and the fix commit is 17:36:57; the working tree is clean at HEAD 55966a6 with the clamp present. Whatever binary produced those numbers does not correspond to the source being audited. Source is authoritative and the amplification is closed.

No residual defect: the doc claims in 6.2/9.4 about bounded queues are no longer contradicted, since a lying header now costs at most the bytes actually supplied.

---

### R2. M0's "the calling state still non-yieldable" is asserted by a tautology

audit `m0-m2`

The reporter's code facts check out (test/dtask_check.c:110 verbatim, L from luaL_newstate() at :279, src/lstate.h:104, src/ldo.c:1004, src/lstate.c:354 incnny with no counterpart decnny outside parsing at src/ldo.c:1177/1188; dtask.c never touches nCcalls and lua_resume writes only the resumed thread's field at src/ldo.c:980). So yes, main_thread_is_unchanged cannot fail for a main-thread caller. But the finding fails this audit's bar on three independently verified counts.

(1) No false statement anywhere. doc/Messaging.md:1719-1720 says the calling state IS still non-yieldable, which is true of this tree — so rule (b) fails outright: the code does not contradict the quoted sentence. And the doc's convention-2 claim is explicitly scoped two sentences later at doc/Messaging.md:1723-1726: "Each of the three mitigations that can fail silently was verified by removing it and watching a named test fail — re-raise removed gives 3 failures, plain `lua_pcall` gives 5, dropped `lua_checkstack` aborts under `api_check`." That tally is checkable and correct — a plain lua_pcall breaks top_level_is_yieldable x2 (isyieldable false; select(2,coroutine.running()) becomes true), hook_reports_the_thread x2 (hook_calls==0, hook_saw==NULL), a_yield_is_loud x1 (a main-thread yield raises via src/ldo.c:1019 instead of reporting YIELD) = exactly 5 — and main_thread_is_unchanged is not among them. The doc never claims that assertion was observed failing; it is the "limitation stated a few sentences later" pattern.

(2) The property is not uncovered. doc/Messaging.md:640-641 assigns it by name to a different oracle: "| `lua.c` / the CLI | Stock Lua. Main thread, non-yieldable top level. | The upstream conformance suite |", and 8.2.1 (doc:663-678) enumerates the guarding sites. test/coroutine.lua:13 is `assert(not coroutine.isyieldable(main) and not coroutine.isyieldable())`, it runs (test/run_tests.sh:79 = "coroutine|run||", not skipped), and it does have discriminating power — it fails under precisely the design 8.2.1 rejected, where ordinary chunks run on a coroutine. test/coroutine.lua:313/:386 and test/test_wait.lua:41 ("the top level is yieldable under --task") cover the movable side. The claim "the second half of M0's first accept clause has no coverage" is therefore false.

(3) Wrong category. "Test passes for the wrong reason" requires green-while-broken. The property genuinely holds, structurally, via src/lstate.c:354; the assertion is redundant, not misleading. That is a test-strength/"consider a better test" critique, which the audit excludes.

Closest surviving concern, which the reporter did not raise: the test file's own blanket comment at test/dtask_check.c:12-13 ("Every case here fails if its mitigation is removed from dtask.c") is over-broad for this case — but the same header immediately narrows to "The three that matter", and this case has no mitigation in dtask.c to remove, so there is no demonstrable wrong result, crash, or false doc sentence.

### R3. M2 and dqueue.c both state that on_full = "block" is refused at declare time; it is accepted

audit `m0-m2`

Quotes check out but the finding misreads the doc's register, and the code half has no failure.

DOC: doc/Messaging.md:1767-1769 is quoted verbatim and accurately. But it sits inside section 13 "Milestones", whose preamble (line 1708) is "Each independently mergeable. Report stripped size deltas at each." — a per-milestone record that supersedes itself in later entries rather than editing earlier ones. The same M2 entry reports "Stripped size 5.9 KB of text at -O3"; M3 reports "queues 10.4 KB"; M4 reports "queues are now **13.6 KB** ... having grown from 10.4". By the reporter's logic M2's 5.9 KB is equally a false present-tense claim. M3, 25 lines after the quoted sentence and in the same section, lists "`queue.wait`, `on_full = \"block\"`, the wait-set protocol" as done. git log -S'refused at declare time' shows the sentence entered in 5155c7f "Add local queues" (the M2 commit) and was never touched; blocking pushes landed later in e2970bc. So the M2 entry reports where M2's line actually fell, which satisfies convention 3 rather than violating it. This is the pattern where the limitation is stated a few sentences later.

NORMATIVE SPEC IS CORRECT: doc/Messaging.md:440 lists on_full as "One of `\"reject\"`, `\"drop_oldest\"`, `\"drop_newest\"`, `\"block\"`", and 444-445 state "`on_full = \"block\"` yields the caller until space is available or the queue is disabled. All other policies return immediately." 7.2 at 545-546 agrees. No reader consulting the API section is misled, so the failure scenario requires taking a milestone entry as the spec while ignoring 6.3, M3, and the two test files the reporter itself quotes as recording the transition.

CODE: the comment at src/dqueue.c:52-58 is quoted accurately and IS stale — dq_opt_enum (173-189) rejects only names absent from dq_full_names, so line 222 accepts "block", and dq_push parks at 579-589. But this is a stale comment: no input or state produces a wrong result or a crash, and the correct behaviour is visible eleven lines below in dq_full_names and again in the push path. It is not a doc sentence, so it cannot satisfy criterion (b), and it has no failure scenario for (a). Under "do not report style / anything whose only support is a reader might be confused", it does not qualify.

Residue worth a cleanup commit but not a finding: e2970bc should have rewritten src/dqueue.c:52-58; the still-accurate part of its reasoning is the host-side note at 1041-1049 that "block" degrades to "full" because a host cannot park.

### R4. dv_resume delivers "timeout" to a queue.wait that set no timeout

audit `m3-m4`

The finding describes code that no longer exists in this tree; it was already fixed by commit 4edaadc "dv_resume: stop inventing a timeout that never elapsed", and every quoted excerpt is inaccurate as of HEAD (55966a6).

1. The load-bearing quote is fabricated relative to the tree. The reporter quotes src/dv.c:629 as `if (diluvium_queue_ready(inst->co, &one, &why) == 0) why = DILUVIUM_FIRED_TIMEOUT;  /* named a handle that is not ready */`. grep -n "FIRED_TIMEOUT" on /home/user/diluvium/src/dv.c returns exactly one hit, line 625, and that is the `if (fired == 0)` branch — the one the reporter itself concedes is correct. Line 629 is `one.timeout_ms = 0;`. The actual body of the `diluvium_queue_ready(...) == 0` branch is src/dv.c:632-651 and it ends with `return DV_IDLE;` (line 651), after a comment that states the reporter's own thesis: "This used to report a timeout, and that was a lie the guest could not detect". No timeout is synthesised for a named handle anywhere in the file.

2. The failure scenario is therefore impossible. Because the branch returns at line 651, it returns before `lua_pop(inst->co, inst->pending)` / `inst->pending = 0` / `inst->parked = 0` / `diluvium_queue_fire(...)`. Naming a live, empty, enabled queue is a no-op: the park's description stays on the stack, `parked` stays set, and the host gets DV_IDLE and may retry. The guest never observes `nil, nil, "timeout"`, so nothing reaches dq_wait_k's timeout return and no "attempt to index a nil value" is reachable. The reporter's own proposed fix ("leave inst->parked set, do not pop inst->pending, and return DV_IDLE, which is what dtask.c's loop effectively does") is verbatim what the code already does.

3. The test claim is also false. The reporter says test/dv_check.c:330 carries the comment "Nothing was pushed, and the queue is empty, so naming it means 'gone'". Line 330 is `eq_i(ws.timeout_ms, 5000, "and the host is told how long it asked for");`, inside `timeout_answer`, which legitimately tests the handle-0 protocol via `dv_resume(inst, 0)`. `closed_answer` now lives at test/dv_check.c:357-378 and was rewritten to exercise a genuinely closed queue: the program calls `queue.disable(q)` before `queue.wait({q})`, `dv_run` returns DV_DONE without parking, and the assertion at 372-373 checks for "closed", not "timeout". Its header comment at 344-354 records the honest line the audit conventions demand — that DILUVIUM_FIRED_CLOSED is unreachable through dv_resume because a parked guest cannot call destroy/disable and the host has no closing call, so the reachable closed path is the synchronous one. A separate test, `a_spurious_resume_invents_nothing` (test/dv_check.c:387-416, registered at line 1252), asserts precisely the scenario the finding claims is broken: park with no timeout, `dv_resume(inst, ws.ids[0])` must return DV_IDLE, the waitset must still be retrievable so the host may retry, and a subsequently pushed real message must be delivered as "ok/hi" — "and the program sees the message, never a phantom timeout".

The cited doc sentences (doc/Messaging.md:450-452 and :465) and dv.h:216-221 are consistent with the code as it stands, so there is nothing left for them to contradict. This is a stale finding audited against a superseded revision. Correction: no defect exists — src/dv.c:632-651 already returns DV_IDLE while staying parked and consuming nothing, DILUVIUM_FIRED_TIMEOUT is produced only for `fired == 0` (src/dv.c:624-625), and both the closed path and the spurious-resume path already have dedicated passing-for-the-right-reason tests.

### R5. dv_load dereferences inst before its own NULL guard

audit `m3-m4`

The quoted evidence does not match the file. src/dv.c:281-290 currently reads:

  dv_status dv_load (dv_instance *inst, const uint8_t *code, size_t len,
                     const char *name) {
    const char *mode;
    int st;
    /* The guard has to come before the first read of 'inst', which it did not: the
       'mode' initialiser dereferenced it two lines above this check, so a host that
       passed NULL crashed rather than being told. */
    if (inst == NULL || code == NULL)
      return DV_ERROR;
    mode = (inst->flags & DV_FLAG_TEXT_ONLY) ? "t" : "bt";

Line 283 (the finding's cited location) is the bare declaration `const char *mode;`, not an initialiser. The NULL guard is at lines 288-289; the first dereference of `inst` is the assignment at line 290, AFTER the guard. The reporter's evidence block, which shows `mode` initialised at its point of declaration above the guard, does not exist anywhere in the tree.

This is not a misquote of live code but a report against a stale revision. Confirmations:
(1) `git blame -L 281,296 src/dv.c` attributes lines 283, 285-287 and 290 to commit 4f529429, whose subject line is "A 5-byte message could make the decoder allocate 131 MB; and dv_load's NULL guard could never fire" - i.e. this exact bug was the named subject of that fix commit.
(2) The finding's proposed fix ("Move the guard above the declaration ... then compute mode") is already precisely what the code does.
(3) A regression test already exists and is wired in: test/dv_check.c:1226 `null_arguments_are_refused_not_dereferenced()`, invoked from main at test/dv_check.c:1250, asserting `dv_load(NULL, (const uint8_t *)"return 1", 8, "=x") == DV_ERROR` and `dv_load(inst, NULL, 0, "=x") == DV_ERROR`. Its comment at lines 1221-1224 records the same history and adds "The rest of the ABI was scanned for the same shape and this was the only one."

Ruled out alternative explanations: `git status --porcelain src/dv.c` is empty, so a concurrent agent did not just patch it out from under the reporter; and `diff src/dv.c .data/dv.c` is identical, so the .gitignore'd `.data/` copy does not carry the old code either. The surviving cross-checks in the finding (dv_new returning NULL at dv.c:232-234/236/246, and test/dv_check.c callers not checking the pointer) are accurate but harmless, because the guard now fires before any dereference.

There is no failure scenario: `dv_load(NULL, ...)` returns DV_ERROR, exactly as the finding asks for.

### R6. The peek registry anchor is claimed as accepted-on but no test can fail if it is deleted

audit `m3-m4`

REFUTED. The anchor code is real and at the cited lines (src/dqueue.c:1083-1084, inside diluvium_queue_peek_bytes at 1065-1088), but every load-bearing part of the finding fails on inspection.

1. THE FAILURE SCENARIO IS SELF-CANCELLING. The finding says: "with the anchor gone, a host that peeks queue q and then calls `dv_queue_pop(inst, q, ...)` ... removes the message from the items table, leaving the earlier pointer aimed at a string reachable from nothing". That outcome is identical WITH the anchor. src/dv.c:357-378 `dv_queue_pop` calls `diluvium_queue_peek_bytes` (dv.c:364, which re-stores the anchor to the same head string), `memcpy` (375), then `diluvium_queue_drop` (376). And src/dqueue.c:1091-1097 is:

  LUA_API int diluvium_queue_drop (lua_State *L, lua_Integer id) {
    ...
    /* Release the anchor first, whatever happens next: ... */
    lua_pushnil(L);
    lua_rawsetp(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, &DQ_PEEKED);

The drop nils the anchor BEFORE it removes items[head]. So after that pop the peeked string is unanchored and unreferenced whether or not lines 1083-1084 exist. The scenario therefore demonstrates nothing about the anchor; it is not "the property it protects".

2. THE PROPOSED FIX WOULD BE A WRONG-REASON TEST — the exact failure mode this project has hit eight times. It asks for: "peek, then remove the message by another route (a second `dv_queue_pop` on the same handle, or a `dv_queue_release` followed by a guest step), then force a collection and read the borrowed bytes back — with the anchor present the bytes must still match." Both named routes go through `diluvium_queue_drop`, which nils the anchor at dqueue.c:1096-1097. So "with the anchor present the bytes must still match" is false: the assertion would read freed memory in BOTH configurations, and any green it produced would be luck. Adopting this fix would add a test that is not evidence.

3. TWO OF FOUR CITATIONS ARE MISATTRIBUTED. The doc sentence "the implementation must anchor the value in the registry between `peek` and `release`" is at doc/Messaging.md:1544-1548, not 1477-1480 (1477-1480 is inside 11.1/11.2's C listing area). And test/dv_check.c:187-209 is not the peek test: 151-209 is `queues()`, and 187-209 is the msgpack-header/DV_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL block. The peek test is `zero_copy()` at test/dv_check.c:212-234 (peek at 223, release at 228).

4. THE DOC SENTENCE IS NOT CONTRADICTED. doc/Messaging.md:1843 reads "and retry; the peek/release zero-copy path with its registry anchor; export" — it says the checks drive that path, and they do: dv_check.c:223 executes the anchor store at dqueue.c:1083-1084, dv_check.c:228 executes the anchor release at 1096-1097, and dv_check.c:230 asserts "release removes it, so peek plus release is a pop". Nothing at 1843 claims the anchor was mutation-tested. Further, src/dv.h:174-178 confines the anchored window to a sequence the header forbids ("**Do not call 'dv_run' or 'dv_resume' in between**"), and src/dqueue.h:148-154 justifies the anchor by precisely that forbidden case — so the residual complaint reduces to "add a mutation test for a belt-and-braces guard on a forbidden sequence", which the audit brief excludes.

I did verify the reporter's negative claim independently: `diluvium_queue_peek_bytes`/`diluvium_queue_drop` have no callers outside src/dv.c (pop/peek/release) and src/dvs.c:1057-1083 (peek, memcpy, release with no Lua in between); grep for `collectgarbage` across test/dv_check.c, dvs_check.c, dsnap_check.c, dtask_check.c, bindings/rust/diluvium/tests/host.rs, bindings/python/tests/test_host.py and bindings/js/test/ returns nothing. So no test discriminates on the anchor. But that alone is a testing gap of the kind the brief rules out, and the finding as filed misidentifies both the hazard and the remedy — which is worse than silence for an audit whose purpose is that the project can trust what it is told.

### R7. dv.h's documented return set for dv_queue_push omits DV_QUEUE_GONE, and three bindings implement the list literally

audit `m3-m4`

REFUTED on three independent grounds.

1. THE CONTRACT DOES DOCUMENT IT. The finding's load-bearing sentence is "the contract test relies on a status the contract does not document." dv.h documents that push returns DV_QUEUE_GONE, twice, in the section that introduces the only mechanism producing it:
   - src/dv.h:269 (in the dv_set_endpoint_handler comment): "A refused bind raises in the guest ... That is different from a push to a bound-but-dead endpoint, which is the ordinary DV_QUEUE_GONE."
   - src/dv.h:305-306 (immediately above dv_endpoint_close, the only function that can produce the state): "Say that an endpoint's far end has closed. Pushes to it answer DV_QUEUE_GONE from then on, immediately."
   The scenario requires a host that "calls dv_endpoint_close and later pushes to that endpoint handle" — i.e. a host reading dv.h:304-313, where the answer is stated two lines above the call it just made. dv_check.c:576 therefore tests a documented behaviour, and M4's claim that the header "suffices on its own" (doc:1836-1837) survives.

2. THE FAILURE SCENARIO IS UNREACHABLE IN ALL THREE ACCUSED BINDINGS, including the one the reporter offers as a live reproduction. GONE has exactly one producer chain: DQ_GONE is set only by diluvium_queue_set_gone (src/dqueue.c:1007), whose only caller in the tree is diluvium_endpoint_setlive (src/dendpoint.c:110) — which returns 0 for any handle where !diluvium_queue_is_endpoint (dendpoint.c:101-102) — whose only caller is dv_endpoint_close (src/dv.c:465). There is no guest-side close: the endpoint Lua library is exactly {"bind", "status", "is_endpoint"} (src/dendpoint.c de_lib). So GONE requires a host call to dv_endpoint_close, and:
   - Rust: bindings/rust/diluvium-sys/src/lib.rs declares no endpoint function at all — its extern block covers dv_abi_version, dv_status_name, dv_new, dv_free, dv_load, dv_last_error, dv_queue_lookup/state/push/pop/peek/release, dv_run, dv_resume, dv_waitset_get, dv_set_notify, and nothing else. No dv_endpoint_allow, no dv_set_endpoint_handler, no dv_endpoint_queue, no dv_endpoint_close. The safe wrapper keeps `raw: *mut sys::dv_instance` private (lib.rs:170) with no accessor. So a Rust host cannot authorise a bind, cannot obtain an endpoint handle, and cannot close one. The reporter's stated reproduction — "push_raw on an endpoint queue whose far end was closed returns Err(Error::UnknownQueue(id))" — cannot be executed with these crates at all; the state it needs is unconstructible from Rust.
   - Python: no endpoint symbol anywhere under bindings/python (so lib.dv_endpoint_close does not exist in the cffi cdef).
   - JS: no endpoint call in bindings/js/src/index.js; the only "endpoint" mentions under bindings/js are in msgpack.js/msgpack.test.js for decoding ext 0x02 as an opaque reference, which is codec-level and cannot mark a queue gone.
   The three missing switch arms are therefore dead branches, not a wrong result. Only diluvium-wasmtime exposes the endpoint surface, and it is the one that has the arm (lib.rs:520) — consistency, not an oversight.

3. LINE NUMBERS DO NOT CHECK OUT IN THIS TREE. The mapping is at src/dv.c:316 ("case DILUVIUM_Q_GONE: return DV_QUEUE_GONE;"), not 312; the test assertion is at test/dv_check.c:576, not 494. Both cited numbers match a stale copy under .claude/worktrees/wf_ddad3a8f-479-8/ (whose dv.c has the case at 312), so the evidence was gathered from a different checkout than the one under audit.

Also, doc/Messaging.md is not wrong: 11.3 lists DV_QUEUE_GONE among the status codes (line 1557), and the M5 record states the tested behaviour explicitly — "a push to a closed endpoint answering `\"gone\"` immediately, from both the guest and the host side, without raising" (doc:1910-1911) — naming from-the-host-side GONE as an accepted, recorded property.
