### R8. "each genuinely non-yieldable context refusing by name" omits the main-thread case

audit `m3-m4`

Quotes and line numbers all check out (doc:1805, doc:768-774, dqueue.c:774-775, dqueue.c:469, test_wait.lua:141-186 with four refuses() cases, no "try --task" string in any test), but the finding fails on four grounds.

(1) The limitation is stated in the same sentence the reporter quotes. doc:1799 reads "Accepted on `test/test_wait.lua`, 58 checks, run under `--task`:". The fifth 8.4 context, "on the main thread when not resumed", cannot exist in a --task run — the reporter uses exactly that fact (test_wait.lua:41 asserts the top level is yieldable) as proof of absence. "Each genuinely non-yieldable context" scoped to a file the doc announces as --task-run is elliptical, not a false statement.

(2) "The fifth is asserted nowhere" is wrong, and the doc says where it is asserted. doc:640 reads verbatim "| `lua.c` / the CLI | Stock Lua. Main thread, non-yieldable top level. | The upstream conformance suite |", and doc:666-673 enumerates the sites. Those sites run in the default runner table with no --task (run_tests.sh: "coroutine|run||", "errors|run||", "locals|run||"): test/coroutine.lua:13-14 "assert(not coroutine.isyieldable(main) and not coroutine.isyieldable())" then "assert(not pcall(coroutine.yield))"; test/errors.lua:399 'checkmessage("coroutine.yield()", "outside a coroutine")'; test/locals.lua:1177 'assert(not st and string.find(msg, "attempt to yield from outside"))'. The reporter grepped for queue.wait, not for the behaviour.

(3) There is no distinct code path to leave uncovered. dqueue.c:774 is one shared "!lua_isyieldable(L)" branch; test_wait.lua proves guard->dq_cannot_park->"not yieldable" four times, and coroutine.lua:13 proves the default main thread is non-yieldable. yieldable(L) is "(((L)->nCcalls & 0xffff0000) == 0)" (lstate.h:104), and the CLI's lua_pcall with k==NULL takes the luaD_pcall/luaD_callnoyield branch (lapi.c:1095), so the bit is set. The composed behaviour follows from two separately asserted halves.

(4) No defect is demonstrated. Today "diluvium prog.lua" calling queue.wait does produce the "(try --task)" message; the failure scenario requires a hypothetical rewrite of the guard's condition ("gate it on lua_getinfo-visible C frames instead of lua_isyieldable"), which is inventing a different implementation rather than removing a mitigation. The substance and the proposed fix are "add a test for correct behaviour", which the audit rules exclude.

Residue worth noting but not the reported finding: doc:2334-2335 says "**Non-yieldable rejection** (`test/test_wait.lua`). One test per context in 8.4" with no --task qualifier, which counted strictly is 4 of 5. That is a different line, contradicts no code, and the missing item's oracle is assigned by doc:640 to the conformance suite that does assert it.

### R9. M3 places the drive loop in docall, and lua.c still says nothing can park

audit `m3-m4`

The quotes are all accurate at the lines given (lua.c:249-263, dtask.c:170-212 and 225-232, doc:1795-1797), but the finding collapses on its own load-bearing claims.

1. The "concrete consequence" is backwards. The finding says `diluvium_task_call` "contains CLI-only clock and deadlock policy at dtask.c:191-208, which the doc attributes to lua.c." It does not. dtask.c:191-208 is only hook dispatch — `int r = (task_wait != NULL) ? task_wait(ws.timeout_ms, task_wait_ud) : ((ws.timeout_ms < 0) ? -1 : 0);` — a function pointer plus a no-hook default that dtask.h:94-96 documents verbatim ("With no wait function installed, an indefinite park is reported as a deadlock and a finite one times out immediately without any time passing."). The CLI's clock and deadlock policy is exactly where the doc puts it: `static int task_waiting (lua_Integer ms, void *ud)` at src/lua.c:221-235, whose own comment at lua.c:206-215 reads "the clock, which 8.3 puts on the host side rather than in the runtime... so it reports a deadlock rather than hanging forever", registered at src/lua.c:789 `diluvium_task_setwait(task_waiting, NULL);` inside the `if (args & has_task)` branch. So the CLI-only policy is in lua.c, injected into a host-agnostic loop — the opposite of the claim.

2. The failure scenario ("concludes the wait-set loop was never built", "looks like a shared helper... not shared with the ABI") is refuted by the doc, which states the reporter's proposed fix in four places. doc:100-101: "The call driver follows that precedent: the mechanism is in `dtask.c` and `lua.c` keeps a delegation plus one option. Do not grow `lua.c` with a new mechanism." doc:632-633: "The driver is `src/dtask.c`". doc:1831-1834: "`dv_run` and `dv_resume` are steps, not loops. The CLI's driver loops because a CLI can afford to block; a host with an event loop of its own cannot... Both drive the same body via `diluvium_task_pushbody`". doc:2057: "an agent parked on `queue.wait`, through dtask.c's real driver, round-trips". doc:2658: "The CLI host had always done this — `dtask.c` loops rather than synthesising a reason". And the M3 paragraph itself, five lines below the quoted line, already draws the split the finding says is missing — doc:1811-1813: "That is the CLI's judgement and not the runtime's: 8.3 puts the clock, and therefore this call, on the host side." Per the instruction to read the whole surrounding section, the section says the right thing.

3. doc:1796 is therefore not contradicted by code. "the drive loop in `docall`'s `--task` branch" names the branch the loop is entered from — src/lua.c:258 `switch (diluvium_task_call(L, narg, nres, base))` inside `else {` at 251 — and the doc's own rule at 100-101 forbids the mechanism living in lua.c. No wrong result, no crash, no false statement.

What does survive, and is not the filed finding: src/lua.c:253-257 carries a stale premise ("nothing in the runtime can park yet... When 'queue.wait' lands, the wait-set drive loop belongs exactly here") — src/dqueue.c:782 `return lua_yieldk(L, 3 + n, 0, dq_wait_k);` means the runtime does park — and dtask.c:225-232 is now unreachable because the `while (status == LUA_YIELD)` loop at 170 exits only via `break`-free fallthrough or an early `return`, so `status` cannot be LUA_YIELD at 225. test/dtask_check.c:190-205 `a_yield_is_loud` does reach DILUVIUM_TASK_YIELD through dtask.c:180-182 rather than the arm its comment names. But the assertions it makes are the true contract (`status == DILUVIUM_TASK_YIELD`, a string message), both paths satisfy them, and no input produces a wrong result or crash. That is three stale comments and one dead arm, none with a demonstrable failure and none contradicting a doc sentence — the audit's own bar excludes it.

### R10. A guest mints and rewrites endpoint references, so 7.3's unforgeability is not structural

audit `m5`

The finding's central mechanism does not exist in this tree, its cited code lines are wrong, and its doc quote is inverted — it quotes a section 17 correction entry's restatement of a *retracted* claim as if it were a live assertion.

1. THE CONSTRUCTOR IS GATED. src/dmsgpack.c:1121-1123, inside the ext 0x02 case, reads verbatim:
     if (!c->trusted) {
       mp_ext_push(L, code, data, len);
       return;
     }
This runs BEFORE the resolver lookup (lua_rawgetp at 1125). The guest-callable entry point mp_decode (the C function behind msgpack.decode) sets src/dmsgpack.c:1342 `c.trusted = 0;                  /* a guest's own string mints no references */`. Only two host entry points set trusted=1: diluvium_msgpack_decode_n at line 1383 ("The host's own entry point, used by queue delivery") and diluvium_msgpack_decode_graph at line 1685 (snapshot restore). So msgpack.decode("\xd4\x029") never reaches de_resolve; it returns an mp_ext_push wrapper (src/dmsgpack.c:567-577) carrying mp_shape_mt, with [2]=MP_SHAPE_EXT and [3]=code. de_ref_bytes (src/dendpoint.c:150-163) compares the value's metatable to DE_REF_MT with lua_rawequal and fails, so bind raises the "never builds one; see 7.3" error — not the "this host binds no endpoints" error the reporter presents as proof of acceptance.

2. THE CITED LINES ARE WRONG. The reporter cites "dmsgpack.c:1065-1072 hands ext 0x02 straight to the installed resolver". Lines 1065-1072 are inside mp_decode_map. The ext 0x02 case is at 1103-1135, and lines 1105-1119 are a 15-line comment describing this exact attack as already found and closed: "'msgpack.decode' is guest-callable, and running the resolver on whatever string it was handed made it exactly that constructor."

3. THE DOC QUOTE IS INVERTED. doc/Messaging.md:1880 actually begins: `**"A reference cannot be forged" was false when written, and is now true.** The claim was: there is no constructor anywhere, its metatable is hidden, and a lookalike is refused by name, so 7.3's "never constructed by the guest" is structural rather than a convention. Two of those three held.` The reporter lifted the subordinate restatement of the withdrawn claim and presented it as the doc's assertion. The same entry then states the fix that is in the code (1892-1896): "The decode cursor now records whether it is reading the host's bytes or a guest's string, and only the host's reach the resolver: the queue delivery path and snapshot restore are trusted, guest-callable `msgpack.decode` is not." The doc even names the same demonstration the reporter re-derived (dv_endpoint_allow, one peer pre-authorised, nothing delivered) and the 9.3-attenuation consequence. Under convention 3 the doc reports this correctly; it is honest, not wrong.

4. THE TEST EVIDENCE IS INVERTED. test/test_msgpack.lua:243 `local ep = msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02x")` exists, but the assertions on 244-249 are the opposite of the reporter's reading: `eq(ep[3], 2, "and the code, so it is plainly not a reference")` and `ok(getmetatable(ep) ~= false, "its metatable is visible, unlike a real reference's...")`. There is no `ref.anything` probe anywhere in test/test_endpoint.lua; line 42 is `local notref = msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02Z")` and 47-51 assert endpoint.bind refuses it with "never builds one". The forgery loop at test/test_endpoint.lua:63-65 explicitly includes `msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02Z")` and `msgpack.decode("\xc7\x06\x02peer-b")` — the precise vector claimed to be unchecked — and requires each to be refused. doc/Messaging.md:1900-1909 records that the two old tests asserted the hole and that both were rewritten.

5. THE REPRODUCTION USED A STALE ARTIFACT. The fix is committed as bbb4509 "Endpoint references could be forged by any guest; close it". dist/diluvium_linux_x86_64 is dated Aug 10 16:49 while dist/diluvium_debug is 17:42, so the binary the transcript was produced against predates the current tree. Convention 1 requires verifying against this tree, and the source contradicts the transcript.

6. THE SURVIVING SUB-PRONG HAS NO DEMONSTRABLE CONSEQUENCE. It is true as a code fact that a genuine host-delivered reference is a plain table built by de_push_ref (src/dendpoint.c:140-146) with the bytes at [1] and a metatable (125-137) carrying only __name and __metatable, so a guest holding a real reference can read and rewrite ref[1]. But exploiting that requires a host that pre-authorises two or more destinations to one guest under guessable byte-names, and no such host exists in the tree: every pre-authorisation site grants exactly one — test/dv_check.c:640 and test/dv_check.c:726 are both `dv_endpoint_allow(inst, (const uint8_t *)"9", 1, 42);`, and bindings/rust/diluvium-wasmtime/examples/sandboxed.rs:82 is a single `a.allow_endpoint(&ref_bytes, token)?;` from one mint. Rewriting ref[1] under those hosts makes de_allowed (src/dendpoint.c:69-79) return 0 and the bind fails. The reporter's stated scenario also requires token "2" to exist, which it never does. No sentence in doc/Messaging.md claims the reference bytes are unreadable or immutable; the only such wording is the C comment at src/dendpoint.c:119-122, and the doc's claim concerns construction, which now holds.

The finding is a rediscovery of an already-fixed, already-documented bug, supported by a backwards doc quote, line numbers that point at unrelated code, tests that assert the opposite of what is claimed, and a stale binary.

---

### R11. endpoint.bind coerces a numeric queue name, the exact mistake 6.3 says is made impossible

audit `m5`

Quotes check out but both bases collapse. (1) Failure scenario refuted: dq_checkname (src/dqueue.c:197-200) accepts ANY string and there is no name-content validation anywhere in dqueue.c, so queue.declare("5") — a fully conforming call through the door the doc blesses — already creates a queue named "5" in the shared names table. endpoint.bind's coercion enables no state conforming code cannot create; nothing wrong is computed, nothing crashes. The claimed confusion also has no call site: names resolve only via dq_lookup (src/dqueue.c:268-274, string-only) and diluvium_queue_find(lua_State*, const char*) (src/dqueue.c:936, reached from dv_queue_find at src/dv.c:325), while handles resolve via luaL_checkinteger/dv_queue_id. No API in the tree accepts a name-or-handle, and test/test_queue.lua:349 asserts the two are not interchangeable. (2) Doc-contradiction refuted on scope: doc/Messaging.md:430-433 sits inside "### 6.3 Lua API", directly after the queue.* signature block, and documents that library's argument checking — src/dqueue.c:193-196 restates the same sentence as a comment on dq_checkname. "### 7.3 Endpoints" (doc:553-570), which specifies endpoint.bind(ref, name) -> id, states no type rule for name. Claiming a contradiction requires extending 6.3's rule to a library 6.3 does not describe, which is inference rather than a quoted sentence the code contradicts; section 17 (doc:2439+) says nothing on it. Verification notes: src/dendpoint.c:201 is exactly "const char *name = luaL_checkstring(L, 2);"; luaL_checklstring in THIS tree (src/lauxlib.c:408-412 -> lua_tolstring, src/lapi.c:415-434) does coerce numbers; diluvium_queue_declare (src/dqueue.c:945-981) does set names[name]=id; and no test/script/binding covers the type of endpoint.bind's second argument. So the inconsistency is real but its only consequence is that a typo at the endpoint door is accepted instead of raising.

### R12. de_ref_bytes returns a pointer into a string the reference table does not hold when slot 1 is a number

audit `m5`

The reporter's code quotes are all accurate, but the finding's stated failure scenario is impossible against this tree, and its "confirmation" was run against a binary that predates the fix that closes it.

WHAT CHECKS OUT
- dendpoint.c:157-163 is quoted verbatim correctly; line 159 is `s = lua_tolstring(L, -1, len);`.
- src/lapi.c:415-434 does convert in place via `luaO_tostring(L, o)` then `luaC_checkGC(L)`, and returns a pointer into the new TString. LUA_NOCVTN2S is commented out at src/luaconf.h:683, so `cvt2str(o)` is `ttisnumber(o)` (src/lvm.h:17) and a number does coerce.
- The reference metatable (dendpoint.c:125-137) really has no `__newindex`, so a reference table's slot 1 is raw-writable.
- The dqueue.c:543-545 warning comment is quoted verbatim correctly.
- de_ref_bytes has exactly one caller, de_bind at dendpoint.c:200.

WHY THE FINDING FAILS
The scenario requires a guest to obtain an object bearing DE_REF_MT. The reporter's route is `msgpack.decode("\xd4\x02Z")`. That route is closed:
- src/dmsgpack.c:1342 — `c.trusted = 0;  /* a guest's own string mints no references */` in the guest-callable decode entry point.
- src/dmsgpack.c:1121-1124 — `if (!c->trusted) { mp_ext_push(L, code, data, len); return; }`, so ext 0x02 never reaches the resolver from Lua.
- src/dmsgpack.c:567-577 — mp_ext_push installs `mp_shape_mt`, not DE_REF_MT.
Therefore `lua_rawequal(L, -1, -2)` at dendpoint.c:157 is false, de_ref_bytes returns NULL, and de_bind takes the ref==NULL branch at line 206. lua_tolstring at line 159 is never reached, so no pointer is coerced or carried forward.

THE EVIDENCE IS FROM A STALE BINARY
The reporter says `pcall(endpoint.bind, ref, "x")` "returns the host-absent error, not the 'never builds one' error." dist/diluvium_linux_x86_64 is dated Aug 10 16:49; commit bbb4509 "Endpoint references could be forged by any guest; close it" landed at 17:22:24. Running both:
- dist/diluvium_linux_x86_64 (pre-fix): "endpoint.bind: this host binds no endpoints..." — reproduces the reporter's observation exactly.
- dist/diluvium_debug (17:42, post-fix, matches HEAD): "endpoint.bind: the first argument is not an endpoint reference. A program receives one in a message and never builds one; see 7.3."
The reporter measured the tree as it was before the forgery fix. This is precisely the convention-2 trap (evidence green/red for the wrong reason), and here it manufactured a false finding.

Furthermore, test/test_endpoint.lua:42-52 already asserts the opposite of the reporter's claim, including `ok(tostring(err):find("never builds one", 1, true) ~= nil, ...)`, so the claimed behaviour is under active regression test.

WHY NOT "certain"
A narrower residual pattern does survive at the same line, which the reporter did not identify: a guest handed a *genuine* reference over the trusted delivery path (src/dmsgpack.c:1383 `c.trusted = 1`, exercised by test/dv_check.c:524-548 where guest Lua does `local _, ref = queue.wait({inb})`) can still raw-write `ref[1] = 12345` before binding, because there is no `__newindex`. That would reach line 159 and produce a popped, unreferenced string. But that is a different scenario from the one reported, and it is not demonstrated: the only GC opportunity between the pop and the use is the `luaC_checkGC` inside luaL_checkstring at dendpoint.c:201 (and only when arg 2 is itself non-string), and freeing a string allocated one step earlier requires the collector to already sit at the end of propagate so a single step can run atomic and flip the new object's white before sweeping the head of allgc. The reporter proved neither that reachability nor any actual free — no ASan run, no crash, no wrong value. Under the audit's bar that is "this looks risky", not a finding.

---

### R13. Token 0 is an unadvertised sentinel: it silently no-ops dv_endpoint_allow and cross-wires two references from the callback

audit `m5`

The finding's load-bearing factual claim is false, and both failure scenarios collapse when traced.

1. THE C ABI DOES REJECT TOKEN 0. The finding asserts: "neither diluvium_endpoint_allow (dendpoint.c:53-65) nor dv_endpoint_allow (dv.c:445-450) rejects it" and "the C ABI the wrapper sits on does not [guard], and no C-level caller is warned." But /home/user/diluvium/src/dv.c:449-455 reads verbatim:

  449: void dv_endpoint_allow (dv_instance *inst, const uint8_t *ref, size_t len,
  450:                         uint32_t token) {
  451:   if (inst == NULL || ref == NULL || token == 0)
  452:     return;
  453:   diluvium_endpoint_allow(inst->L, (const char *)ref, len,
  454:                           (unsigned int)token);
  455: }

The reporter cited dv.c:445-450, a range that stops one line short of the guard, and concluded the guard does not exist. Line 451 rejects token == 0 explicitly. The Rust wrapper's bail!("0 is not a valid endpoint token") (bindings/rust/diluvium-wasmtime/src/lib.rs:575-577, quote confirmed) is a friendlier error on top of a constraint the C layer already enforces, not a guard the C layer lacks.

2. SCENARIO (a) IS IMPOSSIBLE. It reads: "A C host calls dv_endpoint_allow(inst, ref, len, 0). de_allowed returns 0, dendpoint.c:215 falls through, and with no callback installed the guest's bind raises 'this host binds no endpoints...' — false, since the host did register that reference." The host did NOT register that reference: dv.c:451-452 returns before touching DE_ALLOWED, so the table has no entry. With no handler installed, "this host binds no endpoints, so there is nothing for a reference to resolve against" is an accurate message. There is no false statement to the guest. The residue is only that dv_endpoint_allow ignores an invalid argument silently and dv.h:285-300 does not document the constraint — a documentation gap with no wrong result, crash, or false statement, which is below the audit's bar.

3. SCENARIO (b) IS NOT ABOUT TOKEN 0; ITS PREMISE CONTRADICTS ITSELF. I traced the callback path in src/dendpoint.c:198-249 with *token == 0. First bind of ref A: de_allowed → 0, handler runs, returns 1 with *token = 0; diluvium_endpoint_queue(L, 0) does lua_rawgeti(L, -1, 0) → nil → existing == 0 → queue declared → lua_rawseti(L, -2, 0) stores tokens[0] = idA (integer key 0 is a perfectly ordinary Lua table key). Re-binding the same ref A: handler returns *token = 0 again, existing == idA, the same handle comes back — exactly the documented "Binding the same token twice gives the same handle." dv_endpoint_queue(inst, 0) returns idA, so the host finds the buffer. Token 0 in the callback path is fully functional; nothing is cross-wired.

The reporter's misrouting requires the host to return token 0 for two DIFFERENT far ends. But the token IS the identity of the far end by design (dendpoint.c:232-239), so returning one token for two far ends produces the same collapse for token 7 as for token 0 — it is host misuse of a documented contract, not a property of 0. And the stated premise, "a host whose bind callback numbers destinations from 0", does not produce it: numbering from 0 yields 0, 1, 2..., i.e. distinct tokens, which I traced as working correctly. The premise and the conclusion are inconsistent.

4. The project's own host already follows the convention: test/dv_check.c:511 is `return (*token != 0);` — its bind callback treats a computed token of 0 as a refusal, which is the very "treat *token == 0 from the callback as a refusal" the proposed fix asks for.

Quotes I verified as accurate: dendpoint.c:215 `if (token == 0) {`; dendpoint.h:53-56 "Return 1 having set '*token' to whatever the host wants to identify this endpoint by, or 0 to refuse the bind."; de_allowed returning 0 for not-found (dendpoint.c:69-79); diluvium_endpoint_queue returning 0 for nothing bound (dendpoint.c:92-99); lib.rs:576. The quotes are real; the inference from them is not. I also read the whole endpoint section of doc/Messaging.md (lines ~1875-1930) — it makes no claim about token validity that the code contradicts.

---

### R14. Use-after-free: the fault detail is read from the instance the same call just freed

audit `m7`

The finding describes code that does not exist in this tree; it was already found and fixed, and its regression test already exists.

1. The quoted code is not in the file. The reporter quotes `const char *detail = faulted ? dv_last_error(sl->inst) : NULL;` at src/dvs.c:1138. `grep -n detail src/dvs.c` returns hits only at lines 503-545 (inside emit_event's own bounded copy) and 1015-1030 (do_query's status detail). There is no `detail` local anywhere in dvs_step. src/dvs.c:1138 is `dvs_slot *sl = &sw->slots[i];` — the first line of the *wake* loop body, not the stop path.

2. The stop path already does exactly what the finding proposes. src/dvs.c:1192-1203:
      char why[192];
      why[0] = '\0';
      if (faulted) {
        const char *e = dv_last_error(sl->inst);
        size_t i = 0;
        if (e != NULL)
          while (i + 1 < sizeof(why) && e[i] != '\0') { why[i] = e[i]; i++; }
        why[i] = '\0';
      }
      kill_subtree(sw, id, 0);
      if (parent != 0)
        emit_event(sw, parent, what, id, faulted ? why : NULL);
   The error text is copied into a stack buffer *before* kill_subtree, and emit_event is handed `why`, never the instance pointer. No freed memory is read.

3. The comment immediately above (src/dvs.c:1184-1191) documents this exact bug as already fixed: "Copied, not pointed at. 'dv_last_error' returns a pointer into the instance's own error buffer, and 'kill_subtree' below calls 'dv_free' on that instance -- so passing the pointer on to 'emit_event' afterwards read freed memory." `git log --oneline -- src/dvs.c` shows the most recent commit as 55966a6 "A use-after-free on the fault path, and a wildcard that widened a grant"; `git status --porcelain src/dvs.c` is clean, so the tree contains the fixed version. The reporter appears to have read a pre-fix revision.

4. The claim "No test reaches this: every child in dvs_check.c is either killed by dvs_kill ... or parks" is also false. test/dvs_check.c:914 defines `a_faulting_child_reports_a_readable_reason`, invoked at test/dvs_check.c:1038, whose child body is `error(string.rep('x', 4000))` (line 922) — a child that faults while it has a parent to be told — and it asserts the parent decoded a non-empty detail (`ok(saw_fault && strstr(line, "|0") == NULL, ...)` at line 969). The test's header comment at lines 895-913 names this very use-after-free as bug 1 of the three it was written to cover. The reporter's proposed test already exists.

Nothing to fix; the failure scenario is not reachable in the current tree.

---

### R15. emit_event commits to a three-pair map and then drops the third value, poisoning the reader

audit `m7`

The finding describes code that is not in this tree, and its failure scenario is blocked by a check it never mentions.

(1) Misquoted code. src/dvs.c:492 is `}` inside put_uint; the map-header write `*p++ = (char)(0x80 | (detail != NULL ? 3 : 2));` is at dvs.c:538. The load-bearing quote -- that put_str "truncates the length to 8 bits ... (dvs.c:458 `*(*p)++ = (char)n;` after the 0xd9 str8 tag)" -- does not exist. src/dvs.c:474-480 reads: `if (n > 255) n = 255;` / `if (*p + 2 + n > end) { if ((size_t)(end - *p) < 3) return; n = (size_t)(end - *p) - 2; }` / `*(*p)++ = (char)0xd9;` / `*(*p)++ = (char)n;`. n is clamped to 255 and again to remaining room BEFORE the length byte is written, so the header always matches the bytes memcpy'd. Other citations are also wrong: the faulted emit is dvs.c:916 not 870; set_error("the program would not load: %s") is dvs.c:370 not 359.

(2) Earlier check prevents the scenario. emit_event declares `char det[192];` (dvs.c:507) and copies the detail into it before writing the header (dvs.c:532-537): `while (i + 1 < sizeof(det) && detail[i] != '\0') { det[i] = detail[i]; i++; } det[i] = '\0'; detail = det;`. put_str therefore never receives a detail longer than 191 bytes. At the detail-value write p is ~30 (map byte + "event" + what + "id" + id + "detail"), and 30 + 2 + 191 = 223 < 512, so neither the "486..511 bytes writes nothing" nor the "256..485 bytes claims n & 0xff" case can occur. Every other put_str argument is a fixed short literal ("event", "id", "detail", and what in {exited, denied, faulted, spawned, hibernated, gone, status, throttled}). The three-pair map always carries its third value, so dq_take/mp_need never see a truncated payload and the described supervisor-poisoning cascade cannot start. sw->error being 512 bytes (dvs.c:111) is true but irrelevant -- it is clamped on the way in.

(3) Already fixed with a regression test that exceeds the proposed one. test/dvs_check.c:905-909 lists these exact defects as history: "2. 'emit_event' wrote a map header claiming three pairs and then dropped the third when it ran out of room" and "3. 'put_str' wrote str8's single length byte from a size_t, so a 300-byte detail announced 44 bytes and copied 300." The test a_faulting_child_reports_a_readable_reason (test/dvs_check.c:914) spawns a child whose body is `error(string.rep('x', 4000))`, has the guest decode the event with queue.wait and log #e.detail, and asserts `saw_fault && strstr(line, "|0") == NULL` -- the map decoded and the detail survived. That is the test the finding asks for, at 4000 bytes rather than 300/500.

The only residual point is one dvs.c:522-530 already discloses honestly: since det[192] bounds the input, put_str's clamp is defensive-only and "removing either this copy or 'put_str''s clamp turns nothing red." That self-declared redundancy is not a defect and does not meet the finding bar.

---

### R16. implies() with a bare "*" matches everything, so "**" can be attenuated upward into total authority

audit `m7`

The finding's central quote is not in the tree. It claims src/dvs.c:240 reads `if (n > 0 && held[n - 1] == '*')`. Line 240 is a comment line, and the actual guard is at line 250:

  250	  if (n > 1 && held[n - 1] == '*')
  251	    return strncmp(held, want, n - 1) == 0;
  252	  return strcmp(held, want) == 0;

The `n > 1` bound is exactly the fix the finding proposes ("Require a non-empty prefix"), and it is already in place. `git blame -L 237,253 src/dvs.c` dates it to commit 55966a6, "A use-after-free on the fault path, and a wildcard that widened a grant" — the reporter quoted the pre-fix code from 9ddf296.

The failure scenario is impossible as a result. Trace it with the current code:
  - implies("**", "*"): n == 2, so strncmp("**", "*", 1) == 0 → 1. The grant is still permitted, as the reporter says.
  - But the escalation depends on the child's "*" then matching everything, and it does not. implies("*", "queue:secret"): n == 1, so `n > 1` is false and control falls to strcmp("*", "queue:secret") != 0 → 0. Likewise implies("*", "lifecycle") → 0. A bare "*" is now a literal name matching only itself, so dvs_holds(child, "queue:secret") == 0 and dvs_holds(child, "lifecycle") == 0. The child holds nothing its parent could not reach ("**" covers every name beginning with '*', which includes the literal name "*"), so passing "*" on is a narrowing, not a widening. doc/Messaging.md:903-904 is not contradicted.

The finding also asserts the property is untested; it is. test/dvs_check.c:984 is `a_wildcard_cannot_widen_a_grant`, registered in main at line 1039, and it asserts precisely the claimed cases: `ok(!dvs_holds(sw, star, "lifecycle"), ...)` (line 1001), `ok(!dvs_holds(sw, star, "queue:anything"), "nor any queue")` (1003), `ok(dvs_holds(sw, star, "*"), "it grants only the literal name it is")` (1004), the "**" grant path at 1011-1016 including `ok(!dvs_holds(sw, star, "queue:work/a") && !dvs_holds(sw, star, "lifecycle"), "so a child handed \"*\" holds nothing its parent could not already reach")`, plus the "want shorter than the pattern" case at 1018-1019. The test's header comment (975-983) narrates the old bug in the past tense, so this is a fixed-and-regression-tested defect, not a live one.

Nothing survives: the quote is wrong, the line number is wrong, the vulnerability is closed, and the test the finding asks for already exists.

---

### R17. The host-identity stamp of item 6 is not implemented: NULL is passed on both snapshot and restore

audit `m7`

Refuted on three counts.

(1) The cited locations are wrong. src/dvs.c:595 contains nothing quoted; the real calls are dvs.c:631 (size enquiry), dvs.c:641 "if (dv_snapshot(sl->inst, NULL, buf, need, &sl->snaplen) != DV_OK) {" and dvs.c:687 "if (dv_restore(inst, NULL, sl->snap, sl->snaplen) != DV_OK) {" — a uniform 46-line offset from the cited 585/595/641. Doc citations are also stale: "a foreign host stamp is refused" is doc:1940 (not 1914), the M7 record is doc:2148-2153 (not 2119-2127), the capability-gap admission is doc:2140-2146 (not 2114-2121). Only doc:851 and doc:1450-1451 are at the lines given.

(2) The failure scenario is impossible, not merely unlikely. It requires a host to persist the cached snapshot and reload it into another swarm, but nothing exposes the bytes. size_t dvs_cached_size (dvs.h:192, dvs.c:611-613) returns sl->snaplen — a count. The entire public surface of dvs.h is dvs_abi_version, dvs_status_name, dvs_new, dvs_free, dvs_last_error, dvs_root, dvs_step, dvs_alive, dvs_instance, dvs_parent, dvs_kill, dvs_hibernate, dvs_wake, dvs_resident, dvs_cached_size, dvs_push, dvs_budget, dvs_caps, dvs_holds, dvs_may_grant; none yields snapshot bytes. dvs_slot and struct dvs_swarm are defined only inside src/dvs.c (lines 83-107), so sl->snap is unreachable from a host, there is no file/socket I/O in dvs.c, and the buffer is freed in drop_cache (dvs.c:182-186) and at dvs.c:694-696 on wake. The bytes are produced and consumed by the same slot in the same process and cannot cross a host boundary through this layer, so there is no state in which the missing stamp yields a wrong result.

(3) The doc is not contradicted where it reports status, and the code matches the doc's rule for this case. doc:1311-1313: "**The host stamp is asymmetric, and that is the point.** An unstamped snapshot restores anywhere, because a process moving its own state has nothing to check against." The swarm cache is precisely a process moving its own state, so NULL on both calls is the documented-legitimate pairing. The refusal machinery exists and is enforced at the ABI (src/dsnap.c:496-504 compares the header "host" field, src/dsnap.c:551 "the snapshot's host identity stamp is not this host's"), and the stamp was scoped to and delivered by M6 (doc:1935-1936 "host-identity stamp, `dv_snapshot` and `dv_restore`", accept criterion doc:1940). M7's scope line (doc:2149-2150) is "Instance table, `system/lifecycle` and `system/events`, attenuation, budgets, orphan policy, rate limits, snapshot cache with `wake_on_message`" and its three accept criteria (doc:2151-2153) concern supervision, attenuation and wake ordering — none asserts the stamp, so "**All met.**" states nothing false. doc:66 additionally lists "Snapshot authentication. Deferred behind a checkable precondition; see 10.10" under Non-goals, and the sentence the finding leans on sits inside 10.10's paragraph headed "**Authenticate — deferred, behind a checkable precondition.**".

What survives is only that 9.1.2 item 6 (doc:850-851) assigns the stamp clause to this layer and dvs.c implements no stamp — an unbuilt design-scope line whose check, added today, could never fail because the bytes never leave the swarm. That is the unfalsifiable-guard shape the M7 record itself rejects ("A third removal is genuinely unreachable and is now commented as a guard rather than left looking load-bearing", doc:2177-2178), i.e. a not-done design item rather than a false statement or a wrong result. Not certain rather than certain because a stricter reading of doc:1450-1451 as a present-tense claim about built code is defensible; but as filed, both the harm and the milestone-lie are wrong.

---

### R18. An accepted push into a drop_oldest queue is reported as DVS_LIMIT

audit `m7`

The mechanism the finding describes is real, but every leg of its support fails, and its location is wrong.

(1) LOCATION WRONG. src/dvs.c:697 is "  /*" — the opening line of the buffer-drain comment inside dvs_wake, 46 lines from the code quoted. The quoted statement lives at src/dvs.c:743-744.

(2) MECHANISM CONFIRMED, so this is not a fabrication: src/dqueue.c:1040-1054 (count==cap, policy==DQ_DROP_OLDEST) stores the message, advances head and returns DILUVIUM_Q_DROPPED; src/dv.c:315 maps it to DV_QUEUE_DROPPED ("accepted, and the oldest message was evicted", src/dv.h:69); src/dvs.c:744 turns everything that is not DV_OK into DVS_LIMIT. Reachability holds: diluvium_queue_declare stores the policy as an int (src/dqueue.c:967) and dv_queue_lookup -> diluvium_queue_find (src/dqueue.c:936-944) resolves any name regardless of 'exported', so a host push into a guest's drop_oldest queue at capacity does return DVS_LIMIT.

(3) THE DOC QUOTE DOES NOT CONTRADICT THE CODE. doc/Messaging.md:464 `| "dropped_oldest" | Accepted, oldest message evicted |` sits in section 6.4, whose first line is "String statuses at the Lua level. No numeric codes." That table describes what queue.push returns to a guest, and src/dqueue.c:567-577 still does exactly that: `lua_pushboolean(L, 1); lua_pushliteral(L, "dropped_oldest");`. The row is honored. It says nothing about dvs_push, and the doc's only mention of DVS_LIMIT (doc/Messaging.md:2388) is about the wake buffer. The swarm delivery table the code cites (doc/Messaging.md:936-939) covers dead / non-resident-with-wake / non-resident-without-wake and is silent on a resident push into a full queue. So rule (b) — a verbatim doc/Messaging.md sentence the code contradicts — is not met.

(4) THE HEADER QUOTE IS MISREAD. dvs.h:200 "resident -> the queue, DVS_OK" belongs to a table introduced as "the host's side of 8.4's delivery table, and the only call that knows what to do about a non-resident destination" — a routing table about residency, not an exhaustive status contract. Proof from the tree itself: the same table omits DVS_UNKNOWN, which src/dvs.c:742 returns for an undeclared queue name and which test/dvs_check.c:810 asserts is the right answer ("a push to a queue it never declared is unknown, not gone"). If DVS_LIMIT at 744 contradicted that table, DVS_UNKNOWN at 742 would too, and the test says it does not. dvs.h is also not doc/Messaging.md, so it cannot satisfy rule (b) regardless.

(5) NO FALSE STATEMENT AND NO IN-TREE WRONG RESULT. dvs.h:63 defines DVS_LIMIT as "a rate limit or a table full"; in the scenario the table was full, so the status is not literally false. The claim that "not accepted" is "the only reading the enum offers" is the reporter's gloss, not text in this tree. The concrete harm — a retry that duplicates the message and evicts a second one — requires a host that retries on DVS_LIMIT, and no such consumer exists here: dvs_push has no caller outside test/dvs_check.c (grep of src/, test/, script/, bindings/), and bindings/rust, bindings/python and bindings/js contain no reference to dvs_push at all. The internal pushes at src/dvs.c:553 and 709 discard the status entirely.

What is left is a real coarseness at the dv_status -> dvs_status boundary (and it hits DV_QUEUE_DISABLED and DV_QUEUE_GONE the same way), worth a design note, but it is not demonstrated as a wrong result, a crash, or a false statement, and its one verbatim doc quote is from a different API surface that the code obeys. Under this audit's bar that is noise, not a finding.

---

### R19. build.yml has no push/pull_request trigger, so wasi_check.sh, the musl build, the ARM binaries and the Windows build are checked only on manual dispatch

audit `single-point`

The mechanical facts check out (build.yml:14-27 is workflow_dispatch/workflow_call only; test.yml never calls build.yml nor wasi_check.sh/build_linux_static/smoke.lua), but the finding fails on three counts.

(1) Its title is factually wrong. release.yml:18-21 is `on: push: tags: - 'v*'` and release.yml:175-181 is `build: needs: [preflight, test] / uses: ./.github/workflows/build.yml`. So wasi_check.sh, the musl build, the ARM QEMU smoke tests and the Windows build run automatically on every v* tag push, and also on a Release dispatch (release.yml:11-13 documents publish=false as the default "full release rehearsal"). "checked only on manual dispatch" is contradicted by the tree.

(2) No doc contradiction. The finding quotes no doc sentence as false. doc/Messaging.md mentions wasi_check.sh only at lines 680 and 1820, both about repl_eval not being conformance-tested; grepping the doc for build.yml, "Build workflow", "every push", "smoke", "commit-level" returns nothing. build.yml:3-12 states its trigger honestly ("Two ways in: manually ... called by release.yml"), so convention 3 is met. The cited doc:2609 overflow lesson is not a claim about CI timing. doc:2570's principle ("A check that only runs where it cannot be run is not a check") was written about three jobs that failed on every run; these jobs run and were observed green (doc:2239-2241), and wasi_check.sh:5-8 states its own purpose as release-gating.

(3) The failure scenario is hypothetical, not concrete: it begins "A second width-dependent arithmetic bug ... is committed to src/". No inputs, no state, no wrong result, crash or false statement in this tree — it is a CI-trigger/coverage proposal, the excluded "consider adding a test" class, and the proposed fix changes only when jobs fire.

Also overstated: the wasm32 module is built and executed on every push in two test.yml jobs (test.yml:252-254 `cargo run -p diluvium-wasmtime --example sandboxed -- ../../dist/diluvium_wasi.wasm`; test.yml:305-307 `node test/instance.integration.mjs ../../dist/diluvium_wasi.wasm`), so wasm32 build/load breaks are caught at commit level — the gap is coverage depth, not an unchecked property. And wasi_check.sh:74-79 lists 23 tests, not the claimed 22.

---

### R20. The browser artifact's only build step is continue-on-error inside the dispatch-only workflow, and release.yml drops it from the download guide without failing

audit `single-point`

All quotes verified accurate at the stated lines (build.yml:90-95, release.yml:214/239, Makefile:7/185), and the full-tree grep confirms only build.yml:95 and release.yml:239 mention the artifact. The finding still fails on four counts.

(1) No doc/Messaging.md sentence is quoted or contradicted. The claimed contradiction is between a CI code comment and a shell line. doc/Messaging.md never mentions wasm32-unknown-unknown; its only browser text (1673-1674, 1700) is the npm plan, which says "the browser build uses the existing embedded WASI shim" - a different artifact.

(2) build.yml:90 "The browser target is not currently exercised by any release" is not false. In this repo "exercise" means run/execute: the neighbouring steps are "Smoke test the ARM static binaries" (build.yml:123) and "Run the WASI artifact we ship" (build.yml:158), and commit 9cefbf7e's own message says "there was no way to exercise a build without creating a release". Nothing runs libdiluvium_wasm_unknown.a - it is a static archive, and CI has no browser runtime. release.yml:239 lists it in a download table; listing is not exercising.

(3) The failure scenario's payload - "no error anywhere ... Nobody is told the browser library stopped existing" - is factually wrong. script/build_stats.sh:186 is `elif nb is None: rows.append((n, kb(ob), "-", "removed", 0))`; "removed" is truthy so the row passes the `changed` filter at build_stats.sh:194 and prints in the table at build_stats.sh:205-209. build.yml:329-345 ("Compare with the previous release") runs exactly that comparison against the last release's BUILDSTATS.json on every build and appends the output to $GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY. So a vanished artifact is named in the run summary, in addition to the failed-but-continued step GitHub renders inside the successful job.

(4) The behaviour is the deliberate, documented design in the very lines quoted. git blame puts build.yml:90-95 and release.yml:239 in the same commit 9cefbf7e, whose message states "Release notes are now generated from the artifacts that actually exist rather than a hardcoded table", echoed at release.yml:216-219 ("derived from what this build actually produced"). Every row uses the conditional `row` helper, so a missing file produces no row rather than a row pointing at a nonexistent download - the notes never make a false claim. And build.yml:92 already names the not-done part: "Make it blocking once it has been green." Nothing consumes the artifact, so nothing downstream breaks.

### R21. files runs with -e _port=true; the stated reason names one assertion while _port disables four blocks, including the only test of io.popen/os.execute status triples

audit `single-point`

Every quote in the finding is accurate — I confirmed run_tests.sh:84 verbatim, the five _port gates in files.lua (86, 726, 777, 866, 902), the triple assertion at files.lua:816-818 with its table at 805-812, and that `dist/diluvium_debug -e _port=true -e 'print(_port)'` prints true so the 777 block really is skipped. It is also true that 816-818 is the tree's only assertion that io.popen():close() and os.execute() return identical triples (main.lua:67/75 assert only truthiness; test_wait.lua and test_determinism.lua use popen as a mechanism; no C or shell harness touches it). The finding still fails the bar on three counts. (1) No doc contradiction: `grep -rn "_port|popen|os.execute" doc/` hits only doc/manual/manual.of; doc/Messaging.md never mentions _port, files.lua, popen or os.execute, and its run_tests.sh passages (2298, 2596) are explicitly about the six *skip* reasons, while `files` is `run`, not `skip`. (2) Line 84 is not a false statement: each clause is true (line 86 is exactly the stdin-seek assertion, and CI stdin is typically /dev/null, which is seekable, so the assert would fail), and it never claims exclusivity; _port's broad scope is documented at its definition in test/all.lua:25-26, "-- Make true to avoid non-portable tests". The header scopes the column to why a test runs/skips and what extra args it needs, not to a coverage-delta inventory. (3) The failure scenario posits a future edit to code this fork does not modify: `git log --all -- src/lauxlib.c src/liolib.c src/loslib.c` shows only c178900 (rebase onto 5.5.1) and 76c0531 (file reorg), and grep for dv_/DV_/diluvium/hibern/queue in liolib.c and loslib.c returns nothing — l_inspectstat (lauxlib.c:279-280) and luaL_execresult (291) are stock upstream. So the scenario is regression-coverage wishing about untouched upstream code, i.e. "consider adding a test," which the audit excludes; the proposed fix is a comment rewording, and nothing observable changes. The title also says "four blocks" while the body enumerates five.

---

### R22. main is skipped for an accurate reason, but it is the only test in the tree that drives the standalone interpreter, and src/drepl.c has no coverage at all

audit `single-point`

Every factual claim in the report checks out, and that is exactly why it is not a finding: nothing is wrong, only untested, and the doc already says so.

VERIFIED AS ACCURATE (I opened each):
- test/run_tests.sh:91 is verbatim `main|skip||main.lua:82 reads the version with string.match(out, "Lua (%d+%.%d+%.%d+)") and this fork's banner is 'diluvium (lua) X.Y.Z', so 'release' is nil and line 201 compares a string with nil`.
- test/main.lua:82 is `local release = string.match(getoutput(), "Lua (%d+%.%d+%.%d+)")`; src/lua.h:519 is `#define LUA_VERSION "diluvium (lua) " LUA_VERSION_MAJOR "." LUA_VERSION_MINOR`, lowercase, so the capital-L pattern cannot match; main.lua:201 is `if release >= "5.5.1" then`.
- src/drepl.c really holds the behaviour named (`diluvium_repl_load`, drepl.c:36-68: the `"return " .. code` retry and the `EOFMARK`/`DILUVIUM_REPL_INCOMPLETE` continuation signal), reached from src/lua.c:687. I grepped the whole tree for `diluvium_repl_load` / `diluvium_repl_complete`: the only callers are src/lua.c, src/dline.c:472, src/wasm_stubs.c and src/wasm_stubs_unknown.c. No test, no shell check, no workflow. test/all.lua:158 does `dofile('main.lua')` but all.lua is referenced only by test/packtests, not by the Makefile or CI. So the coverage gap is real.

WHY IT IS STILL NOT A FINDING:

1. No concrete failure scenario exists in this tree. The stated scenario is "a regression in src/drepl.c ... ships green" — a hypothetical future defect, not specific inputs or state producing a wrong result, a crash, or a false statement today. The report concedes the point itself: "The reason is true." Nothing in drepl.c is shown to be broken.

2. The proposed fix is literally to enable/add a test ("accept the fork banner in the version match at main.lua:82 ... or extract the -i/_PROMPT/-E cases into a Diluvium-owned shell check"). That is the "consider adding a test" category the audit rules exclude by name.

3. The doc is not contradicted — it states this exact fact, so convention 3 is satisfied and there is no false statement to correct. doc/Messaging.md:2595-2604, verbatim: "**A stated reason goes stale silently; an evaluated one cannot.** Four of the six skip reasons in `test/run_tests.sh` were factually wrong. ... `main` blamed static linking; it actually fails because `main.lua:82` reads the version with `string.match(out, \"Lua (%d+%.%d+%.%d+)\")` and this fork's banner says `diluvium (lua) X.Y.Z`, so `release` is nil. Three of the tests pass once the sentence stops being believed". "Three of the tests" — api (run_tests.sh:69), attrib (:70), literals (:89) — is precisely the doc naming main as the one that does NOT pass. The report quotes no doc sentence the code contradicts, because there isn't one. The doc also records REPL non-coverage elsewhere: line 680-681, "`script/wasi_check.sh` drives the standalone binary, not `repl_eval`, so the WASM REPL path is *not* conformance-tested", repeated at 1820-1821.

4. The title contains a factual overstatement. "it is the only test in the tree that drives the standalone interpreter" is false: test/run_tests.sh:18 sets `BIN="$REPO_ROOT/dist/diluvium_debug"` and runs all 48 table entries through it, and test/interrupt_check.sh:22/:44 drives the same binary directly. What main uniquely covers is CLI-option and REPL-stdin behaviour, not "the standalone interpreter".

Net: an accurate observation about where test coverage stops, already written down in the design document, with no demonstrable defect attached. Reporting it as a finding would crowd out real ones.

---

### R23. guard_not_glibc skips literals on macOS, but every libc-sensitive assertion in literals.lua is inside a self-skipping locale block

audit `single-point`

The reporter's quotes are accurate (test/run_tests.sh:59-64 and :89 are verbatim; literals.lua:299/302/309/313/320-322/327 are as described; .github/workflows/test.yml:102-104 is macos-latest with experimental: false), and the mechanism is real: macOS has no `ldd`, grep matches nothing, `!` inverts to success, so literals is skipped there. Everything the finding builds on top of that is false against this tree.

1. The stated harm is contradicted by test/strings.lua, which runs unguarded on every platform (test/run_tests.sh:97 `strings|run||`). The finding says "the only environment where a non-glibc formatting difference could ever be observed is the one it refuses to run in." strings.lua asserts exact float formatting on macOS on every CI run: strings.lua:226-241 `%q` float round-trips (`checkQ(math.pi)`, `checkQ(0.1)`, `checkQ(math.huge)`); strings.lua:316-346 the whole `%a`/`%A` block, which asserts the ISO C shape `"^%-?0x[1-9a-f]%.?[0-9a-f]*p[-+]?%d+$"` AND full precision `assert(tonumber(s) == n)` over 13 values, plus the libc-divergent inf/nan spellings at 334-339 — and those run, because `_port` is set only for `files` (run_tests.sh:84), not for strings; strings.lua:277-285 `%.99f` round-trips; strings.lua:217 `assert(tonumber(string.format("%f", 10.3)) == 10.3)`. A non-glibc float-formatting difference is observed on macOS today, guard or no guard.

2. "macos-arm64 ... never lexes a single literal" is plainly false. `./test/run_tests.sh --list` yields 47 tests; only literals is guard-skipped on macOS, so 46 still run and lex literals (strings, math, constructs, pm, utf8, ...). The specific regression class the failure scenario invents — "char signedness in the `$`/`??` lookahead" — has dedicated unguarded tests that run on macOS: test/test_fstrings.lua (run_tests.sh:103), test/test_nullco.lua (:107, 54 occurrences of `??`, e.g. `assert((nil ?? "fallback") == "fallback")` at line 11), test/test_safenav.lua (:108). Also test.yml:525-535 compiles every test/*.lua with `luac -p`. So no lexer regression is "green on both CI legs" by absence.

3. The "false statement" prong dies on the full sentence, which the skeptic brief tells me to read. run_tests.sh:59-61 reads: "It passes on glibc, which is what CI runs; musl and macOS are simply unverified, and claiming otherwise in either direction would be inventing a result." It names macOS as unverified in the same sentence, so it does not misrepresent the matrix as Linux-only, and the guard produces exactly the state the sentence claims. The reason column at :89 ("other libcs are untested rather than known-bad") is likewise accurate.

4. The finding is labelled false-doc-claim but quotes no sentence of doc/Messaging.md. The nearest doc sentence, Messaging.md:2598 ("`literals` blamed musl while passing on glibc, which is what CI runs, so it was skipped everywhere for a problem occurring somewhere this project does not test"), is a historical account of the prior prose reason and is not contradicted; musl gets only a smoke run (build.yml:133 runs script/smoke.lua under alpine), never the suite.

5. The proposed fix rests on an unestablished premise. "literals.lua:299 already self-skips the only locale/float-format-dependent block" only holds if `os.setlocale("pt_BR")` fails on macOS. macOS ships many locale definitions preinstalled where a minimal Ubuntu image ships almost none, so the gate may well open there and run precisely the hex-float `tonumber("  +0x.41  ")` (:302), `" 0x.1 " + " 0x,1"` (:309) and `%q` float round-trip (:313) assertions the guard hedges. Neither the reporter nor I can run a Mac here to settle it — which is the guard's stated point.

What survives is only "the guard is broader than its stated reason strictly needs", a coverage-widening preference whose failure scenario is hypothetical. The audit brief excludes exactly that.

---

### R24. The obfuscation-audit job is named advisory while being blocking, and the summary it writes on a real leak calls the leak expected

audit `single-point`

The quoted code exists but the finding does not meet the audit's bar, and its cited lines are partly wrong.

WHAT IS TRUE: .github/workflows/test.yml:538 is `name: Obfuscation audit (advisory)` (537 is the `obfuscation-audit:` block header). The job has no `continue-on-error` (the file's only one is line 94, on `suite`), runs unconditionally on push/PR, sets `STRICT: 'true'` at line 562, and at 611-613 does `elif [ "$STRICT" = true ]; then echo "::error::..."; exit 1`. So the job is blocking while named advisory. Line 605 does say `echo "Expected while handoff findings 3 and 4 are open."` while the env comment at 557-561 says those fixes landed — and the fixes really are in the tree (`taintSecureStrings` at src/ldump.c:213-240, `D->encrypted = f->is_encrypted` at src/ldump.c:403), so 605 is stale and the `::warning::` at 615 is dead code because STRICT is hardcoded.

WHY IT IS NOT A FINDING:
1. The headline claim is about a job's NAME. The audit rules explicitly exclude naming. And the mislabel errs in the CONSERVATIVE direction: the job enforces more than its title promises, so no plaintext leak can reach a merge because of the word "advisory". The dangerous inverse (named blocking, actually advisory) is not what is here.
2. The stale-summary half has no reachable failure state in this tree. Line 605 executes only when `leaked=1`, which requires a future regression in ldump.c's scramble — not "specific inputs or state" against the current code. The audit forbids findings whose support is a hypothetical.
3. Even in that counterfactual, enforcement is untouched: the same step emits `::error::secure chunk contains plaintext constants` (612) and exits 1 (613), so the job is red with an error annotation sitting next to the stale sentence. The claimed harm is that a reviewer might read one obsolete line in a job summary attached to an already-failing job.
4. The declared kind `false-doc-claim` is unsupported. grep of doc/Messaging.md for "handoff finding", "obfuscation", and "Obfuscation audit" returns zero hits; the only "advisory" hit is line 1314, about snapshot stamping. No doc sentence is contradicted, so requirement (b) fails outright.
5. Two of three cited locations are wrong: `STRICT: 'true'` is at 562, not 556-557; the stale echo is at 605, not 606. The convention demands exact line numbers.

At most this is message/comment hygiene in a CI file — the excluded class — not a defect with a demonstrable wrong result, crash, or false statement about the code.

---

### R25. The swarm layer never stamps a snapshot with a host identity, though 10.10 and 9.1.2 item 6 say it does

audit `doc-claims`

Code quotes check out (src/dvs.c:631/641/687 pass NULL; src/dsnap.c:503 maps NULL to ""; no stamp in dvs.c; test/dv_check.c:1032 is the only stamp test), but the finding fails both prongs of the standard.

(1) The failure scenario is unreachable. src/dvs.h exposes no way to export or import a slot's cached bytes — the only cache accessor is `size_t dvs_cached_size (dvs_swarm *sw, dvs_id id)` (dvs.h:192), which returns a length. In dvs.c, `sl->snap` is assigned only at :647 from the malloc'd buffer dv_snapshot fills at :641, read only at :687 by dv_restore, and freed at :184/:694; there is no file I/O in the file (the only `open` hits are diluvium_mp_open cursors at :776-821). So dvs-produced bytes cannot leave the process and foreign bytes cannot reach dvs_wake. The reporter's scenario is explicitly conditioned on a host that "implements the file layout §15's open question 1 anticipates" — and doc:2382-2390 records that as not built: "Still open after M7, which built the cache in memory: a snapshot is a `malloc`'d buffer on the instance's slot, and there is neither a file layout nor an eviction policy". That is a feature the doc names as not-done, which the audit rules exclude. It also explains the reporter's own admission that there is no mitigation to remove: their proposed dvs_check case (restore a slot's bytes under a different swarm) cannot be written without inventing new API, and a swarm-side stamp today would be dead code that no test could exercise.

(2) The doc does not make a false milestone claim. doc:1440-1450 is design/planning prose in §10.10's "three jobs" ("Validate — required in M6", "Authenticate — deferred", and the very sentence ends "That is roughly twenty lines" — an effort estimate for work, not a record). §9.1.2 is titled "What the swarm layer owns, exhaustively" — a layering assignment. The part of the doc that must report where the line falls is §13, and it is accurate: the M6 record (doc:1933-1941) lists "host-identity stamp, `dv_snapshot` and `dv_restore`" with accept criterion "a foreign host stamp is refused ... All met", and test/dv_check.c:1032-1060 genuinely enforces it three ways (same stamp restores, "host-beta" gives DV_SNAPSHOT_MISMATCH, NULL host gives DV_SNAPSHOT_MISMATCH). The M7 record (doc:2148-2154) enumerates the swarm deliverables — instance table, lifecycle/events, attenuation, budgets, orphan policy, rate limits, snapshot cache with wake_on_message — and claims no stamp; its accept criteria never mention one. doc:2140-2146 further states "so `dvs_hibernate` stamps nothing ... Within one swarm that is sound — the set lives in the slot and the slot outlives the swap — but a snapshot written to disk and restored elsewhere would come back with whatever the restoring swarm grants", i.e. the record already scopes the layer's soundness to one swarm and names the off-machine case as unhandled.

What remains is that a design sentence at doc:1450 reads present-tense about a swarm-side stamp that in fact lives at the dv_* ABI — a wording nit with no failure scenario and no contradicted milestone claim, which is below the reporting bar here.

---

### R26. Milestone check counts have drifted: M7's "66 checks" is 81, M6's dv_check "128" is 152

audit `doc-claims`

The quotes are at the lines given, but the finding fails on both its evidence and its interpretation.

1) Its headline number is wrong. `dist/dvs_check` prints "92 checks, 0 failed", deterministically — three consecutive runs plus `dist/dvs_check_asan` all give 92, not the reported 81. Both binaries postdate their sources (dist/dvs_check 17:43, test/dvs_check.c 17:40) and `git status --porcelain` is empty, so this is HEAD. The report's "understates its layer's coverage by 15 checks" is itself off by 11.

2) The doc's counts are per-milestone snapshots by construction, not claims about the current file — the same file appears at three different counts inside section 13: doc:1999 "`test/dsnap_check.c`, 71 checks", doc:2020 "`dsnap_check.c` is 112 checks.", doc:2055 "`dsnap_check.c` is 140 / checks"; likewise doc:1837 "Accepted on `test/dv_check.c` (73 checks" vs doc:2098 "128 checks". At most one number in each set can describe the file as it stands, so the document's own convention is unambiguous: the figure records where that milestone's line fell. Section 14 (the test plan, 2305-2380) carries no counts at all, so there is no place the doc offers these as live figures.

3) Each number was true when written, which is what convention 3 asks. `git log -S` places "66 checks" in b428373 (M7), where `git show b428373:test/dvs_check.c` has 96 ok/okf call sites against 127 today (+31 static, +26 executed); the growth comes from post-M7 commits (c39649c, a175d57, bbb4509, 4edaadc, 4f52942, 55966a6), every one of which section 13's M8 material narrates by name. Same pattern for the rest: "128 checks" entered in ba2f8f7, after which four commits touched test/dv_check.c; "139 checks" in 531cec2, after which 39b41e7 and bbb4509 touched test/test_msgpack.lua; "287 checks" in 5155c7f, after which e2970bc touched test/test_queue.lua; "140" in 9383e62, after which ba2f8f7 touched test/dsnap_check.c.

4) The stated failure scenario therefore does not hold. A later session comparing doc to run sees a document that already reports the same suite's count rising milestone by milestone, and a runner that prints its own current total. The proposed fix's second half ("let the runner's own summary be the current figure") describes the status quo.

One residual, recorded because it is the only part that survives checking and drift cannot explain it: doc:1958 "`test/dshim_check.c`, 82 checks." was written in a9ce2ac, `git diff a9ce2ac HEAD -- test/dshim_check.c` is empty, and the binary prints "87 checks, 0 failed" deterministically (plain and ASan). No ok()/okf() call sits inside a loop in that file and the DILUVIUM_SNAP_* enum its one bounded loop walks (src/dshim.h:183-186) is unchanged since a9ce2ac, so the executed total has always been 87 — a 5-off miscount in the record. It changes nothing about M6's behaviour, its acceptance criteria, or where its line falls, so it is bookkeeping below this audit's bar, not the drift story the finding tells.

---

### R27. "open question 1 is closed" collides with §15's item 1, which is open

audit `doc-claims`

Refuted on four independent grounds.

(1) The cited location is wrong. The finding anchors at doc/Messaging.md:1594 and quotes "doc:1594-1595", but the sentence "with symbol prefix `dvs_` (confirmed; open question 1 is closed)" is at lines 1592-1593; line 1594 is "vtable:" and 1595 is blank. Checked doc/Messaging.md plus the three .claude/worktrees/*/doc/Messaging.md copies — all place it at 1592, so this is not a stale-revision artifact.

(2) Decisive: the reporter quoted §15's numbered item 1 (doc:2383) and stopped before the reconciliation six lines later. doc/Messaging.md:2392-2397 reads verbatim: "Closed since the first draft: the swarm prefix is `dvs_` (11.5); default `on_full` is `\"reject\"` (6.3); the queue name separator is `/`, structural only (6.6); `inbox` and `outbox` are auto-declared (6.6); the capability check signature is `(capability, resource, context) -> allow | deny` with the token model opaque (8.5)." This is the classic "doc states it a few sentences later" case. The claimed failure scenario — a reader concluding either that the dvs_ prefix is unsettled or that the cache question is closed — is impossible for anyone reading §15, the only place item 1 exists: §15 states in plain words that the prefix is `dvs_` and closed (cross-referenced to 11.5) and that the cache question is "Still open after M7".

(3) The claim that the citations of questions 3, 4 and 5 are "not resolvable from this document" is demonstrably false. Each resolves uniquely by matching §15's section cross-reference to the section containing the citation, and by content: :505 (in §6.6) "open question 4" -> "`inbox` and `outbox` are auto-declared (6.6)", matching :503-505 verbatim; :512 (in §6.6) "open question 3" -> "the queue name separator is `/`, structural only (6.6)", matching :512-513 down to the phrase "structural only"; :795 (in §8.5) "open question 5" -> "the capability check signature is `(capability, resource, context) -> allow | deny` ... (8.5)"; :1592 (in §11.5) "open question 1" -> "the swarm prefix is `dvs_` (11.5)". The doc already cites the questions by name with section pointers — which is the reporter's own proposed fix.

(4) The collision reading is self-defeating. Every in-text citation has the form "open question N is closed", and §15's paragraph is headed "Closed since the first draft". A question described as closed cannot be a live entry in a section titled "Open questions", so the numbering is transparently the first draft's. Internal evidence agrees: the numbers cited as closed are 1, 3, 4, 5, leaving 2 as the survivor that §15 renumbered to 1 — a renumbering §15 discloses rather than conceals.

Separately, the finding's code evidence is accurate but supports the doc rather than contradicting it. Verified independently: src/dvs.c:42 "#define DVS_MAX_PENDING\t16"; :97-98 "uint8_t *snap; size_t snaplen;" on the slot; :182-186 drop_cache doing "free(sl->snap);"; :753-754 "if (sl->npend >= DVS_MAX_PENDING)\n    return DVS_LIMIT;". All confirm §15 item 1 is a true statement about where M7's line fell.

What remains is a two-line citation error in the finding plus an editorial preference about citing superseded numbering in a section that also reconciles it by name — a naming/style matter with no false statement, no wrong result, and no code contradiction, i.e. inside the audit's do-not-report set.

---

### R28. make snap_fuzz cannot fail: every mutant is refused by the payload digest before the thread record is decoded

audit `new-c`

The mechanism is real but the finding is not. Verified from source: src/dsnap.c:365-374 puts a SHA-256 of the payload in the header, src/dsnap.c:512-525 verifies it, and src/dsnap.c:1784 runs that check before the payload body at 1795-1798, so unrestamped payload mutants are refused as SNAP_CORRUPT before ds_buildthread (src/dsnap.c:1573) calls checkframes (src/dshim.c:401, quote accurate). I also confirmed no test references checkframes or any DILUVIUM_RES_* code (only src/dshim.c, src/dsnap.c, src/dshim.h and .data/worktree copies match tree-wide), so the coverage gap is factual.

It fails both required legs anyway. (1) The statement called false is not false: script/fuzz_snapshot.py:16-17 claims only that the mutated fields are the ones checkframes validates, which is true against src/dshim.c:412-484; it does not assert reachability. (2) The limitation is stated in the same docstring a few sentences later — script/fuzz_snapshot.py:25-33 records "With neither the payload digest nor the frame consistency checks: 4 crashes. * With the consistency checks alone ...: 3. * With the digest as well: 0." That is the mitigation-removal convention 2 demands, performed and recorded, showing the author knew the digest dominates in the shipped configuration and measured the frame checks with it off. (3) src/dsnap.h:136-137 states the reporter's headline verbatim as intended design: "The fuzzer measures the first [the digest] and the field checks are what its findings were fixed with." doc/Messaging.md:2531-2536 repeats it in section 17. (4) No doc sentence is contradicted: doc:1941 "the fuzzer reports no crashes. **All met.**" is true, and doc:2366-2371 "assert refusal, never a crash ... 430 mutants, 0 crashes" is true — the mutants are refused; neither claims the shipped run exercises the frame validator. What remains is a test-strengthening request ("have fuzz_snapshot.py rebuild the header ... then re-measure"), which this audit excludes.

---

### R29. dqueue.c's header says on_full = "block" is refused at declare time; it is accepted

audit `new-c`

The factual observation is accurate but it does not meet the audit's stated bar, and its supporting argument is partly false.

VERIFIED TRUE: the comment at src/dqueue.c:52-58 is quoted correctly and sits at those lines. dq_full_names (src/dqueue.c:64-66) lists "block" at index 3; dq_declare (src/dqueue.c:203-265) calls dq_opt_enum(L, 2, DQ_FULL, dq_full_names, DQ_REJECT) at line 222 and contains no rejection of index 3; dq_push implements DQ_BLOCK by parking at src/dqueue.c:579-589. The comment is stale.

WHY IT IS NOT A FINDING:

(1) Neither branch of the bar is satisfied. Branch (b) requires "a sentence in doc/Messaging.md quoted verbatim that the code contradicts" -- the reporter concedes the doc is correct, and it is: doc/Messaging.md:444 states "`on_full = \"block\"` yields the caller until space is available or the queue is", and doc:1795 lists it under M3. doc:1767's "`\"block\"` is **refused at declare time**" lies inside the entry opened at doc:1763, "**M2: queue subsystem, local only** — done.", so it is an accurate historical record of where M2's line fell, which is exactly what convention 3 demands. Branch (a) requires "specific inputs or state leading to a wrong result, a crash, or a false statement" -- no input or state produces any wrong result here. Behaviour is correct and covered.

(2) The stated failure scenario is speculation about what a future human reader might conclude and then do. That is the human-factors form of "this looks risky", which the brief excludes.

(3) The finding's central support is demonstrably false. It claims "The comment is the only in-file statement about the policy's status and it says the opposite of the code." In the same file, src/dqueue.c:1041-1046 reads: /* A host cannot park, so "block" degrades to "full" here rather than / silently accepting. 7.5 requires that accepting a message be O(1) and / never wait ... */ -- and dq_push at 579-589 implements the parking path with a 7.2 citation and a dq_cannot_park guard. A reader of dqueue.c cannot come away believing block is refused at declare time when the same file implements its parking and dq_declare visibly lacks any rejection.

(4) The reporter did not grep the header. src/dqueue.h:23-26 carries the identical stale sentence -- "This milestone is local only: no host boundary, no endpoints, and no waiting. 'queue.pop' never yields, and 'on_full = \"block\"' is rejected at declare time rather than silently treated as something else -- see the note on that in dqueue.c." -- along with two further stale claims in the same breath, since endpoints exist (DQ_ENDPOINT at src/dqueue.c:46, diluvium_queue_is_endpoint at src/dqueue.c:985) and the host boundary exists (src/dv.h). Only three sites in src/ use "this milestone" prose at all (src/dshim.h:39, src/dqueue.c:54, src/dqueue.h:23). So this is one instance of M2-vantage prose left in a file header, i.e. comment hygiene spanning at least two files, not a discrete defect at one line.

(5) The behaviour is tested and the transition is explicitly recorded where a maintainer would look. test/test_queue.lua:75-76: "-- 'block' was refused while the wait-set protocol did not exist; it is / -- accepted now." test/test_queue.lua:80-83 asserts declare succeeds and info reports "block". test/test_wait.lua:233: "-- Accepted now, where M2 refused it at declare time." test/test_wait.lua:294-299 exercises a blocking push that nothing can drain in a subprocess and asserts a reported deadlock. The non-yieldable path is diagnosed by dq_cannot_park (src/dqueue.c:464-471) from src/dqueue.c:583-584. Nothing about the policy's status is undocumented in the places that govern behaviour.
